# Ex Machinis Game Design Manual

_Ex Machinis_ is an online multiplayer space simulation designed to expose players to programming and robotics. In the game, which takes place against the backdrop of the real-time solar system, players program remotely piloted spacecraft to mine, manufacture, and trade goods and services.

The game uses augmented reality to project a parallel universe upon our own. In the game's re-imagined post-war history, world leaders embraced space exploration and colonization as a means of protecting mankind from the global threats that emerged in the wake of the second world war.

This wiki is used to organize the project, which began in August 2017.  It contains multiple sections which describe the game's design and function in great detail. These are the blueprints we follow when coding and creating content.

This document lays out the plans for developing Ex Machinis, an online space simulation, which promotes the acquisition of programming skills.

> **This is the working design manual.** Update it in the same pull request as the code or game data it describes, so the two never drift apart. The game data (resources, protocols, their effects, queries and where minerals can be mined) is generated from spreadsheets in [`game-engine/sql/game-data`](../game-engine/sql/game-data/): change a spreadsheet, run `build_game_data.py`, and commit the regenerated `game-engine/sql/game_data.sql` together with this manual. The query list in [Appendix A](#Appendix-A-In-Game-Queries) is written by that script.

## System Overview

### Plot
Ex Machinis takes place in a parallel universe where technology has advanced to the point where humans are using drones to mine and exploit the riches of the solar system. In an effort to spur extraterrestrial industries, governments are awarding citizens (new players) one-time grants of money and drones to take part in this economic expansion.

Given the high cost and risk of manned spaceflight, economic expansion into the solar system has come to be dominated by fleets of remotely controlled drones. Drones can accelerate and decelerate much faster than manned spacecraft allowing them to move quickly between distant points in space. Furthermore, drones require far less infrastructure than manned missions and fleets of drones can be effectively managed by a single programmer.

Much of the physical labor of drones is accomplished by smaller self-propelled bots which specialize in localized tasks like mining, construction, and repair. Drones are typically outfitted with pods, which launch and control bots that are equipped to perform specific tasks. Fleets of drones drive the modern economy by mining, refining, and manufacturing goods and technologies which benefit a largely Earth-based human population.

Although faster than light communications exist, they are much too expensive and cumbersome to be broadly deployed.  Therefore they are generally only used to span the gaps between the earth and deep space trading hubs.

[Return to the TOC](#Table-of-Contents)

### Key Concepts
Ex Machinis is an augmented reality space simulation in which players program and control interstellar drones in real time. Consequently, the simulation is able to adhere to several key principles:
* Continuous gameplay. The player’s drones always remain in play and continue to follow their programs even while the owner is offline.
* Realistic time scales.  Although advanced technologies are able to compress the time it takes for drones to travel long distances and complete industrial activities, it is still expected that many activities will span the course of hours and days. A master time scale lets game time run faster than real time to keep the game playable (see [Game time runs on a master clock](#Game-time-runs-on-a-master-clock)).
* Communications limited to the speed of light. Communications with distant drones is not instantaneous.

[Return to the TOC](#Table-of-Contents)

### Functional Components
Ex Machinis has several functional components which operate independently to support gameplay.

#### Mail Handler
**Removed (#367 step 5).** There is no email: no mail server, no `/get-mails` mail processing, no registration, no welcome or report emails. Commands reach drones only through the `commands` table (a release on the website, or the engines' own resumes and restarts), and replies go to `drone_messages`. The engines' email code stays but is off (`EMAIL_ENABLED` defaults to "0"). The rest of this section, and the meta-commands below, describe the original email game.

The Mail Handler application runs continuously in the database to process incoming and outgoing emails.
Incoming emails will be entered as new events in the Central Database and directed to the appropriate drones.
Outgoing emails, generally originating from in-game drones, will be pulled from the events log at the appropriate time and forwarded to the owner of the drones.

[Return to the TOC](#Table-of-Contents)

#### Game Engine
The Game Engine is a server based application that runs constantly in the background to retrieve, process, and update game data in the Central Database.  GE activities include:
* Processing the compiled FORTH routines that are running in each of the drones.
* Resolving the in-game actions taken by each of the drones in response to their programs.
* Routinely updating the orbits of celestial data with information with data from a public registry.

[Return to the TOC](#Table-of-Contents)

#### Physics Engine
The Physics Engine is a small C++ application, which runs continuously in the background to compute and record the cartesian coordinates of orbiting bodies.

#### Event Engine
The Event Engine is a small C++ application, which runs continuously in the background to identify breaking events, check for errors, and determine which drones will become aware of these events.

[Return to the TOC](#Table-of-Contents)

#### Game Database
The Game Database is a server-side database that holds all game-related data. The database facilitates communications between the Mail Handler, Game Engine, and other applications that are running on the server.  It is also a rich source of data for players and their drones.

The schema starts from `game-engine/sql/create_db.sql` and `game-engine/db-backup/exmachinis_tables.sql`. Every later change is a numbered migration in `game-engine/sql/migrations/`, applied in order by `game-engine/sql/migrate.sh`, which records each in the `schema_version` table. In Docker the `migrate` service runs it on every start, before the website and engines, and then loads `game_data.sql`; CI runs it before the tests. The engines no longer change the schema themselves. See the migrations folder's README for how to add one.

[Return to the TOC](#Table-of-Contents)

<a name="The-Rules-of-Play"></a>
## The Rules of Play

This section is the whole rulebook of the live game in one place: what a player can do, what it costs, and the numbers behind it, each with the issue that set it. The live game has played by these rules (the new rules, #635, behind `FORGE_NEW_RULES`) since the reset of 14 November 2026 (#642). The sections further down explain how each rule is built; this one says what the rules are. When a rule or a number changes, change it here in the same pull request.

<a name="Rules-in-eight-lines"></a>
### In eight lines
1. You write Lua code; your drones run it on their own in a shared, live world.
2. Found a startup for free: the bank lends it a drone and some cash. Run up to five; close one at any time and its debt is cancelled. Failing is free.
3. A startup trades only with the House (the game's own traders at each exchange), and you can change its code whenever you like.
4. When it holds its debt and a little more, you may mature it: its code is locked for good, it can trade with everyone, and you get its 100 shares.
5. Your account starts with 5,000 credits that can only buy shares. Each game day a matured company pays half its profit to its shareholders.
6. Trade shares with players within a published range, or sell them to the bank for their cash-out value, paid 30 game days later at the lower of the two values.
7. All code is public. A matured company can't change, but it can react to prices and orders, so read its code before you buy.
8. You're ranked by your wealth, and by what you made in today's season (a real day).

<a name="Rules-accounts"></a>
### Accounts
- **An account** is a name (3 to 32 letters, digits, spaces, dots, dashes or underscores, public), a password (at least 8 characters) and an e-mail address (private, one account per address). One address may make 3 accounts an hour (#636).
- **Verifying** the address (a link sent to it, good for 2 real days) brings the account its **5,000 starting credits**, which can only buy shares, and only at or below the bank's selling price (#636, #638). An account must be verified to mature a startup.
- **A forgotten password** (#910): Log in's **Forgot your password?** e-mails a link to the account's verified address, good once, for an hour; the reply is the same whether or not the account exists. A new password, set this way or by a teacher, ends the account's other sessions and cancels its API token.
- **Class groups.** A verified teacher makes a group of up to 40 pupils' accounts, named after the group, with passwords shown once. Pupils have no e-mail address, get their starting credits at once, trade shares only with their own group, and have their own boards (#636, #640).
- **Accounts only.** Only an account founds a startup (#871: guest startups ran outside the five-startup limit, so a player could race many and claim only the winners). A guest's startup founded before then keeps its claim code (#637) and can still be claimed into an account. A company name that only looks like another's (case, spacing, invisible characters, 0 for O, 1 for l) is refused (#872).
- **Credits.** An account's credits are what its shares earn it (dividends, sales to players and the bank). They buy shares at any price within the range.

<a name="Rules-startups"></a>
### Startups
- **Called startups since #855**, the usual name for a new company; they were seedlings before. The rename covered the website, the API (`/startups`, `startup` fields), Lua (`startup_status()`), the engine, the database (`companies.startup`, `startup_buys`, `startup_skims`; migration 0087) and the settings (`FORGE_STARTUP_*`), with no old names kept.
- **Every release is a startup** (#637). The bank lends it its drone, valued at **1,000** (about what the House pays for a kit), its **3,000** starting credits and a **200** fee: it owes **4,200** (#658). Its drone starts at Earth with 100 fuel and no equipment; a kit costs 2,000.
- **At most 5 startups** an account; a closed startup's slot reopens **1 game day** after it closed.
- **It trades only with the House** (and its own company's drones): any other trade fails with **-16**. It plunders nothing, and its wrecks can't be plundered.
- **A daily cap on buying.** From the House at one exchange, a startup buys at most **a tenth of what the House has on sale** there (its sell order's units above the stock it keeps) in a game day, and always at least 5 (#777), counting what it has already bought there today; past that, **-17** (#651, #663). Selling to the House has no cap.
- **A cap on credits.** A startup holds at most **twice its starting debt** (8,400): its debt without push fees, so paying for a push doesn't raise the cap (#684). When a sale would take it over, the excess goes to the bank (#672), so there is no point delaying maturing.
- **Reprogramming.** Its player pushes new code from their account or the API; its drones start over on it with fresh variables, keeping their holds and places. A drone on a trip finishes it first: its new `launched()` starts where it arrives (#675). **The first push is free; each after it adds 100 to its debt** (#658). `POST /check` previews the fee.
- **Closing.** Its player can close it at any time: its drones become wrecks with empty holds, nobody can plunder them, and its debt is cancelled.
- **Starters as startups.** The Miner, Refiner and Maker starters work as startups: they sell straight to the House, post no standing orders, and launch no copies until matured. As a startup the Maker checks the House's supply before its first move and buys its metals where a unit costs least, counting the round trip shared over what its tenth-a-day cap lets it buy there (#671, #688). The Trader needs other drones to trade with, so it isn't offered under the new rules.

<a name="Rules-maturing"></a>
### Maturing
- **When.** Once a startup's credits, less any processor tax it owes, cover its debt and **500 more** to work with, a verified player may mature it (#664). `can_mature` says so; it can turn false a moment later if the drone spends, so the player tries again.
- **What happens.** It repays its debt; its code is **locked for good** (nobody can push to it, ever); it trades with everyone; and its player gets its **100 shares** (#667; 1,000 until then).
- **After.** A matured company runs until it fails. Its starters launch copies once matured, which spends credits on hulls and kits and lowers its cash-out for a while. Two of one player's matured companies may trade with each other: their dealings are flagged.
- **On the website** (#697). A company's title carries a **Startup** or **Matured** badge and its owner. A live startup's page shows its **Road to maturity**: a bar of its credits against its debt and the mature mark (debt + `forge.startup_mature_margin`), with its credit cap marked (`Startups::creditCap`), a plain-words warning when it has stalled, and, for its logged-in owner only, **Mature it** (when it can), **Push new code** (the first is free) and **Close it**, through the account's startup routes. "Owes the bank" is among its figures. Under the new rules the home page pitches the startup, with its four steps' numbers taken from the config, the economy at a glance and a short feed in which every name is a link and fuel purchases at a place are grouped; the old rules keep the old home page.
- **Time to mature** (round 71, 1,800×, #740): a Miner with `hour_value` 4 (its default since) 55 to 70 game days; an unchanged Maker about 70; no unchanged Refiner within 80. Round 68 measured 33 to 50 for the Miner and 66 for the Maker on an emptier world.

<a name="Rules-shares"></a>
### Shares, the bank and dividends
- **100 shares** a matured company (#667). Companies never trade shares under the new rules: every share action by or for a startup's or a player's company fails with **-18** (#638).
- **Cash-out value**, a share: the company's credits, plus its goods and equipment each at its best listed House bid anywhere, averaged over the last 7 game days (#685), plus each working drone's hull (1,000, falling to 0 over 365 game days; nothing for a drone with no fuel that has bought none in 3 game days), less its debts, over 100.
- **The bank buys** any player's shares at cash-out value. The shares go to it at once; it pays **30 game days** later, at the lower of the cash-out value then and on the day of the sale.
- **The bank sells** the shares it holds at its **selling price**: cash-out value plus what a share was actually paid in dividends over the last 10 game days (#673; `forge.bank_premium_days`, 30 until #781). A company that has just matured sells at cash-out value.
- **Players trade** with standing orders, in whole shares at whole credits, between **0.8 and 1.25 times** the bank's selling price (rounded). A new order trades at once against the best orders it meets, at their price; the rest stands. The book lists only orders within today's range. A buy order can't commit more than the account can pay; starting credits pay only at or below the bank's selling price (#665).
- **Dividends.** Each game day a matured company pays **half its profit** (a quarter until round 72, #747) (sales after tax less purchases, after making up earlier losses; never more than its credits) to its shareholders by their shares: players' accounts, and the bank for the shares it holds (#667; half until then). The day it matured isn't paid.
- **Pegged orders (#766).** A share order may follow `buys_at` or `sells_at` ± up to 25% (`player_share_orders.peg_ref`, `peg_offset_bp`, migration 0074); `PlayerShares::repeg` on the scheduler's keeper moves each to its price now, posting it again so it trades with what it meets.
- **The history within a disk budget (#813).** Archiving (#694) moves events between two tables on one disk, so it never freed space, and the live disk filled (#811).
  - **When it prunes.** `HistoryBudget::pruneIfDue`, on the keeper once a real minute, runs when the disk's effective use is over `forge.history_budget_percent` (60 since #821, 70 before: room for a backlog like #818's and for `OPTIMIZE TABLE`, which needs about a table's size free). Effective use is the used space less the `data_free` MySQL holds inside `events_archive` and `observations`: InnoDB reuses deleted rows' space instead of returning it, but only for that table's own rows (one file a table), so the space the archiver frees in `events` doesn't count (#827: after #818's backlog it read 58% while the disk filled to 88.5%).
  - **Rebuilding.** A history table holding at least `REBUILD_MIN_BYTES` (1 GB) free and more free than its rows take is rebuilt (`OPTIMIZE TABLE`, online), handing the space back: by the keeper itself, once a real day at most, while its rows take under `AUTO_REBUILD_MAX_BYTES` (1 GB; a rebuild copies them and needs that much free); a bigger one is logged, for `php artisan forge:history-rebuild`. The clock API lists them in `disk.rebuild`.
  - **What it deletes.** The oldest `events_archive` rows (5,000 a batch) and their `observations`, after summing each drone's into `drone_days` (migration 0080: events, failed, actions, first and last game time, a row a drone a game day).
  - **How much.** It takes off about the excess at the tables' bytes an event, within 15 real seconds (40 above 80% used).
  - **Gameplay is unaffected.** The engines and queries 803 and 804 read `events` alone, never the archive.
  - **What else reads the summaries.** `EventHistory::firstGameT` (a hull's age) reads `drone_days` too.
  - **The safety net.** The deploy clears Docker's build cache over 85% and stops over 90%. `GET /api/v1/clock` gives `disk`.
- **Finished actions go after 30 game days (#944).** Nothing deleted from `actions`, and live it had grown to 44.6 million rows, 3.07 GB, the largest table. `ActionsPrune::runIfDue`, on the keeper after the history budget, deletes the actions that are finished and old, for up to 15 real seconds a call.
  - **Finished.** No event of the action is left in `events`: none is pending, and the archiver (#694) has moved every one, which it does only once the action has nothing left to settle and the event is no longer the latest of its kind. So nothing the engines or drones' queries read (they read `events` alone: queries 803, 804, 820, 821 and 845 join `actions` for the acting drone and protocol; the order book, a flight, the wake heap's held resumes) loses its action. And nothing else holds it: a tool lock (`tool_locks.action`, migration 0082), a drone waiting for it (`agents.waiting_action`, which also holds back its resume and gives `perform` its result), a share request on its way (`share_requests.action`), a station offer not yet cleared (`station_offers.action_id`, which pays only for an action that exists and wasn't aborted).
  - **Old.** Its last event in `events_archive` is more than `house_rules.actions_keep_days` (30; 0 turns it off; 30 with no `house_rules` row; migration 0093) game days before the clock's now. An action with no events left anywhere (pruned by the history budget, or only lock and unlock events, which the archiver drops) goes once its row's real `timestamp` is that many days old: at least as many game days at any clock speed of 1x or more.
  - **How.** It walks the table by id, a window of the next 2,000 actions that exist, from where the last call stopped (a cache key) to a ceiling: the action of the first event in `events` at or after the cutoff (by `events_game_t_idx`). Each window's finished actions are found with a plain read (no locks; anti-joins on `events.action_idx`, `tool_locks_action`, `share_requests`' key, and `archive_action` for the last game time) and deleted by id, 500 at a time, so the only row locks are on rows the engines no longer touch. No index is added. A pass that reaches the ceiling starts again from the oldest a real hour later (what stays below it, mostly the latest events' actions, isn't read again every minute). In a test of 500,000 actions in memory it read about 37,000 a real second; live, cold, perhaps a fifth of that, so the 44.6 million backlog clears in about 2 to 13 hours (a call every real minute or so).
  - **The events engine's purge is gone.** `db_purge_old_actions` deleted actions with no event in either table more than `ACTION_EXPIRATION_DAYS` real days old, once a pass, in one locking DELETE that read every action older than that (by `timestamp`, then sorted) to find a few: nearly every live action still had archived events.
  - **Who loses what.** The archive's readers: a drone's timeline and post-mortem list actions from `actions`, so they end where the kept actions do (a wreck's page says its actions are no longer kept, and "Its last action was" drops out); the events page shows an archived event without its acting drone and protocol ("-"). Everything else that joins `actions` reads `events` alone (whose actions are never deleted), or the pending events' (`(SELECT aborted FROM actions WHERE id = e.action) != 1` and the like), or a drone's waiting action.
  - **Space.** InnoDB keeps the freed space for new actions, so the table stops growing. `actions` is in `HistoryBudget::TABLES`, so the clock lists it in `disk.rebuild`, the keeper rebuilds it once its rows take under a gigabyte, and `php artisan forge:history-rebuild --table=actions` does it at once.
- **The account trading script (#768).** One Lua script an account may run (`player_trade_scripts`, migration 0075). `TradeScripts::runAll` on the keeper runs each switched-on one at most every `forge.trade_script_every` (120) real seconds: PHP writes the share market and the account as a Lua table (`TradeScripts::market`, `::lua`), `bin/lua-trade` (`game-engine/src/lua_trade.c`, built with the engine and in the website's image) runs the script's `trade(market)` in a sandbox (safe libraries only; 20 million instructions, 32 MB, 20 actions and 50 log lines a run; a 5 real-second timeout from PHP) and prints its actions, which `TradeScripts::apply` puts through `PlayerShares` under the player's own rules. What it keeps in `memory` round-trips as a Lua chunk. Starters: `examples/traders/value.lua`, and `payback.lua` for classes (#779: buy under `max_payback` game days' payback; since #832 1,000, as live shares pay back in hundreds to thousands of days where 40 bought nothing, and sell only a company about to end, dissolving or wound up within 10 game days: on "at risk" it would have sold about a million credits of Juno's shares to the bank because one drone had no fuel). Market rows carry `winds_up_in`; past 20 actions a run the log says so once. Its orders carry `player_share_orders.by_script` (migration 0079), shown on the book as "(script)"; `repeg` keeps it.
- **The Trading desk (#779).** `/market/trade` (`TradingDesk::build`): the watch list (the stock exchange's board, the account's holdings first, with health pills), one company's book as a ladder between the band's ends with the bank's two prices among the orders, an order ticket (since #845 the shared one below), the account's shares with their gain (`TradingDesk::gains`: worth at cash-out less the average price paid for the shares bought, plus the dividends they paid; shares from maturing cost nothing), and its trading script's latest run. Round 72's players traded across the stock exchange, a company's page and the account page.
- **The House's own loop (#764).** The scheduler calls `/house/{key}` every real second (`HouseMarket::refreshNow`, one run at a time under a cache lock), so the House's orders follow the market's half-day price steps and its imports and shipments within a game half hour at 1,800x. On the tick, behind its other work, they lagged by 2.5 game hours or more: Mars Metals bid 10 against a listed 21, rods read 0/0 once filled, and `left` jumped far above `daily` after a shipment. The tick reposts them only when the loop hasn't run for 30 real seconds.
- **Amber is for links (#905).** Links, buttons, the active tab and controls that open something are amber; labels, step numbers and a selected card's tick use the cyan accent (#4cc9f0), so amber always means "you can press this". E-mails keep amber as the brand mark.
- **One order ticket (#845).** `partials/share-ticket.blade.php`, on the desk and every matured company's page. **Buy or sell now:** `ShareTicket::quote()` lists the routes, players' standing orders within the band (not your own, same market only, no more than each seller holds), an open offering at `offerPrice` and the bank (at `sells_at` while it holds shares and the company isn't dissolving; buying at `buys_at` with no limit), in price order with players first at a tie, and fills N from them; `ShareTicket::now()` trades it in one transaction: players' part through `PlayerShares::order(..., fillOnly: true)` at the worst planned player price (nothing left standing), then the offering and the bank in the quote's order, any shortfall from a player who traded first going on to them; fewer than planned throws and rolls everything back. The page sends the quote's worst price as `limit`, so a moved market can't trade worse than shown. **Name my price** posts an order (fixed or pegged) and quotes with `book=1`. The desk's ladder rows fill the ticket, holdings have Sell, and orders have Move (`PlayerShares::moveOrder`: re-placed under its old id and `game_t`) and Cancel. Round 73's players had three pages and five forms, each with its own price, and only scripts traded. The single-route forms stay on the company page under "Each route on its own".
- **The bank's companies.** At the reset the bank started three matured companies (Bank Miner, Bank Refiner, Bank Maker) running the starters, and holds their shares, so players had shares to buy on day one (#642).

<a name="Rules-trading"></a>
### Trading, orders and the House
- **Trades.** A drone trades by pulling from another drone's sell order or pushing into its buy order, at the same place. The seller pays **5% sales tax** (`sales_tax_permille`, 50).
- **The House** keeps a station drone at each exchange (Earth, the Moon, Mars) that buys at three quarters of a good's value and sells at half as much again, so players beat it both ways; its prices move by place and over time (booms, slumps, shortages and gluts), and it restocks during the day (#380, #630). It bids at most **80% of a kit's price** for the kit's parts at any exchange, so kits can't be flipped (#657). Kits cost 2,000.
- **The minimum order life.** A drone of a startup or a matured player's company can't withdraw, cut or worsen an **open** standing order for its first **12 game hours**: a buy order lower or wanting fewer, a sell order dearer or keeping more back, or either withdrawn, fails with **-19** (#639, #650, #662). The hours run from when the order took its present terms; posting the same order again doesn't restart them. So a distress bid can't lure rescuers and vanish.
- **Messages are private.** A broadcast is read only by the sender's company's drones; to ask other companies for help, post a high buy order.

<a name="Rules-costs"></a>
### Running costs and failure
- **Fuel.** Actions burn fuel; a drone that can't pay for an action's fuel runs dry and becomes a wreck. A failed transfer costs no fuel. `fuel_floor(n)` keeps a reserve.
- **Processor tax.** A company pays **15 credits a million instructions** its drones run (#544), rising up to **10 times** while the game clock runs slowed, for companies that think the most (#620).
- **A fleet cap.** A company may have at most **8 working drones** (`house_rules.fleet_cap`, #682); a launch that would take it past them fails with **-20**, and the kit stays aboard. The cap is checked again when the launch lands (#746: a company once reached 9): refused then, the event's outcome is -20 and the launcher gets its kit and the clone's 20 fuel back. The House's own companies aren't capped. In round 69 matured starters copied themselves without limit (454 drones, one company 103), and the clock sat at its floor: no startup could mature in an hour. Migration 0064 scrapped the drones already past the cap, newest first (`wreck_cause` "fleet cap").
- **One count of players' drones (#716).** The map, the places table and `GET /api/v1/places`, the dashboard and the home page's tiles count the same drones, `World::playerDrones()`: every drone but the House's station drones and a closed startup's (which left the game). The map counted every agent (154 working against the dashboard's 148). The map's labels sit on the side away from the Sun.
- **Figures that agree (#716).** The dashboard's matured-profit chart and its companies table both sum `CompanyEarnings::daily` for the matured companies still trading, from the day each matured (the chart had counted ended companies, so 30 days read −49,440 against the table's −36,683). Its players are the public's accounts, as `/players` ranks them (a class group's pupils have their own boards). Its spreads read `World::orders(type, limit, true)`: only orders whose drone is still where it posted them (`events.place`) and isn't flying, so a buy order left at Mars by a drone on its way to Phobos makes no crossed book. Today's bars are faded and hatched (the day isn't over). A company page computes cash-out once (`PlayerShares::prices($id, $cash)`) for its tile, its Shares paragraph and the bank's prices; its chart's dots are trades between players only; and it says when the world began recording cash-out (the first `company_days.cash_out`). The Stock exchange's last price is the last trade between two players, with its age, and a premium or discount only within `WorldController::PREMIUM_DAYS` (7) game days; it sorts by yield. `/companies` leaves out the House (`companies.house`), and an ended company's age stops at `eliminated_at`. The events page, whose search took longer than a page view on the live world, was off the World menu until #726 made it fast and put it back.
- **Broke companies.** A company with no credits for **50 game days** in a row dissolves: its drones become wrecks, holds and all, for anyone to plunder (#547). The House never does.
- **Crashes.** A drone whose `launched` crashes restarts from its code, waiting longer each time; **five crashes in a row** make it a wreck.
- **Plunder.** A drone at a wreck plunders it once and takes **half** of each resource aboard (`plunder_share`, 50). Startups plunder nothing and their wrecks can't be plundered.
- **The clock** runs at **1,800 times** real time (a game day every 48 real seconds), slowing as far as 60 times when the engines fall behind; at the floor the world is full and new startups are refused until there is room.

<a name="Rules-boards"></a>
### Boards, the digest and dealings
- **Wealth**, a player's: credits and starting credits, plus shares at cash-out value. `/players` ranks players by wealth, and by what they made in today's season, a real day (UTC) (#640, #775: it replaced growth this month over a 5,000 floor, whose "+47,390%" meant nothing to a newcomer). The account page keeps personal records.
- **The digest.** On the account page (and `GET /account/digest`), what happened since the player last looked: each company's sales and purchases, what a startup sent the bank over its cap, dividends, the bank's payments, same-owner dealings and the news (#640, #655, #672).
- **Dealings.** A company's page lists the companies it has traded with or plundered, and flags those with the same player behind them (#639). The bank's own companies aren't flagged.
- **The company boards** (net wealth: growth since the start, without hulls or debt) and the lineage map stay as they were.

<a name="Rules-dashboard"></a>
### The economy dashboard
- **What it is for** (#698): whether the market is busy, whether companies make money, what trades where, and who is winning, on one page (`/dashboard`, in the Market menu) and in the API (`GET /api/v1/dashboard`). The home page shows its banner and tiles.
- **Worked out on the server** (`App\Services\Dashboard`), in the background (#723). A view always gets the last one worked out and never rebuilds it; the scheduler's keeper (`/keep/{CRON_KEY}`, once a real minute, `Kept::keepAll`, #738) rebuilds each kept figure once it is its TTL old (`Dashboard::TTL`, 120 real seconds), under a cache lock, so never two at once. Only the very first view, with nothing kept, waits. From #723 to #738 a view started the rebuild after the response with Laravel's `defer`, but this app's HTTP kernel (`App\Http\Kernel`, the old style) has no `InvokeDeferredCallbacks` middleware, so no rebuild ever ran: in round 71 the dashboard was 480 game days old and the stock exchange frozen. Each kept page says when it was worked out ("as of"), and warns when that is more than 10 real minutes (`Dashboard::STALE_REAL_SECONDS`), or a game day, behind the clock (`Dashboard::staleNote`, #751); the API's dashboard carries `as_of_t`, its game time. It was cached for 60 seconds and rebuilt inside the view that found it expired: on the live database a rebuild took about 90 s, mostly cash-out's lookup of each drone's first event over 19 million events (now kept for good, as it never changes), so views kept MySQL busy and the game slowed during round 70. Since #726 the pattern is `App\Services\Kept` (`Kept::get($key, $ttl, $work)`), which the stock exchange page uses too.
- **The stock exchange page (#726)** took 4.5 to 7 real seconds on the live world: 3,000 queries a view, several a company and two or three a drone. Now `WorldController::sharesPage()` keeps the whole page (`Shares::exchange()`, `PlayerShares::board()`, `PlayerShares::trades()`) with `Kept` for `WorldController::SHARES_TTL` (60) real seconds, and a rebuild does a handful of queries for every company at once: `PlayerShares::cashOuts($ids)` (cash-out for many companies: goods grouped by company, one fuel lookup for all their drones, one `events` lookup for the drones with none aboard, each drone's first event kept for good), `dividends30Many`, the bank's holdings and the holders in one query each, the sparklines from `company_days` and the last trade between players from `player_share_trades` (`MAX(id)` by company) in one each; only `CompanyHealth::of` is still a company at a time. `cashOut($id)` is `cashOuts([$id])`, and the dashboard and the daily snapshot use `cashOuts` too. On a local world of 40 matured companies, 3,400 drones and 2.4 million events a cold view took 4.4 s and 3,157 queries, a warm one 2.6 s; now a view takes about 3 ms and no query, and a rebuild about 2 s (460 queries, nearly all `CompanyHealth`), by the keeper.
- **The events page (#726)** (`/events`, `GET /events/search`) never answered on the live world: it counted, sorted and filtered the join of `observations` (a row an event) with `events`. It now takes the latest `HomeController::EVENTS_WINDOW` (500) events the observer has seen, by index (`observations (drone, game_t)` up to now, and for a drone the events at its place since it arrived, `events (place, game_t)`), joins their details, filters by keyword and pages them in PHP: under 200 ms on that local world, where the old search ran past two minutes.
- **Companies list (#726).** `/companies/search` counts a company's working drones (`agents`) and its wrecks (`wrecks`) apart, leaving out station drones; the company page lists its working drones and folds its wrecks into a `<details>`.
- **The norm** is the average game day over up to 60 game days before the last full one. The banner calls the market quiet below half of it, and when the clock is slowed it says the pace, the engines' lag and processor tax's multiplier, from the same data as `/api/v1/clock`.
- **Counting.** Drones are players' drones: the House's station drones are left out, and so are a closed startup's, which leave the game. A matured company's profit counts from the day it matured. Yield is a share's dividends over 30 game days against its cash-out value.
- **Charts** are inline SVG drawn in the page by one small kit (`resources/views/partials/ex-charts.blade.php`), in the colours of the price charts: blue, orange, green, gold, pink and violet in that fixed order, checked for colour-blind readers against the dark panel. Losses are red, wrecks grey. Every chart says where its numbers come from and has tooltips; the page is one column on a phone, with no sideways scroll.
- **Event marks** (#720). Round 70's players read the biggest moves in the charts (the 8-drone cap's scrap on 14 Sep 2032, the outage of 11 to 20 Oct 2032) as their own failure or as bugs. Every day chart takes a `marks` option (`ExCharts.frame` draws them behind the data; `dayHover` labels them over it and adds them to the day's tooltip; the price and volume chart uses `ExCharts.markLayer` the same way): a rule change is a dashed amber line, a repair a dashed green one, an engine restart a dashed blue-grey one (#750), an outage a grey band, and a company's own event a dotted grey line. Labels go in the first of up to three rows (one on a short chart, two on a phone) where they touch nothing, or are left off; the tooltip still has them. The marks come from `App\Services\WorldEvents::marks()`: the world's events are one list, `config/world-events.php` (each with a key, kind, label, sentence and issue, and a fixed game day, the migration that brought it in, whose `schema_version.applied_at` is turned into game time through `game_clock_history`, or the reset's time), worked out once a real hour (cached); a company's own come from `companies.matured_t` and `eliminated_at`, `code_pushes` and `player_share_trades` (by its `(company_id, game_t)` index, grouped by game day: any sale to the bank, and 10 or more shares bought from the bank or traded between players), never from `events`. The API serves both: `GET /api/v1/world-events` and `GET /api/v1/companies/{id}/events`. Add an entry to the config when a rule change, outage or repair lands. Engine restarts aren't in the config: the game engine adds a row to `engine_starts` (engine, real time, game time) each time it starts (`db_record_engine_start`, shard 0 only; migration 0084 notes the deploy's own start, since a deploy starts the engines before it migrates), and `WorldEvents::restarts()` makes one `restart` mark a game day from it, the newest 200 days; the cache key carries the table's latest id, so a restart shows at once (#750: drone pages said "The engine restarted" and the charts didn't).
- **Credits balance (#937).** Credits are held in `companies.credits` (the House included), `users.credits`, `users.share_credits` and the old share market's `share_commitments.credits`; everything else moves them between those holders. What creates or destroys them writes a row to `credit_flows` (migration 0094; + created, − destroyed) in the same transaction as the change:
  - **triggers** for what the engines and the website already record: a company founded with credits (`company_founded`), a company that ends with some (`ended_<end_cause>`: closed, dissolved, drones, wound up), sales tax (`company_taxes`), a startup's skim (`startup_skims`), processor tax paid (`compute_bills`), and an account opened or deleted with credits;
  - **`App\Services\CreditFlows::record`** in the website for the rest: a startup maturing (its grant repaid), a push fee, starting share credits on verifying, the bank selling shares (`bank_sold_shares`), paying for the ones it bought (`bank_paid`, also at a wind-up), a rebate, and the bank's own dividends (`dividend_to_bank`). A wind-up now takes what it pays its holders out of the company's credits before it ends, so only the remainder is a flow.
  - **The check.** Once a game day the website's tick (`HouseMarket::tick` → `CreditCheck::checkIfDue`, under a cache lock) reads what every holder has and the flows not yet counted in one consistent snapshot (REPEATABLE READ) and records in `credit_checks` the change in what is held, the flows (created and destroyed, and by kind) and the difference, which is 0 when credits balance. A difference is logged as a warning ("Credits don't balance (#937)"). The first check, and the first after a world reset (`CreditCheck::restart`), is a baseline. Counted flows are kept for the latest 50 checks, checks for the latest 2,000 and always when they found a difference.
  - **Where it shows:** `GET /api/v1/clock`'s `credits`, the dashboard's "Do credits balance?" card, and `php artisan forge:check-credits` (checks now; exits 1 when they don't balance), which the smoke test runs from a baseline after its last hand-set credits. The old drop stations' budget (`station_state`, unused since protocols 31 and 32 went) isn't a holder: were they used again, their trades would show as differences.
- **Steadier share lines** (#720). The company page draws cash-out a share (and the bank's selling price) as a 7-day rolling average over the days with a figure, with a band from the least to the most cash-out on those days (`lines()` takes a series `band`); the tooltip gives the day's own figure. The stock exchange's 30-day cash-out sparklines share one percent scale, each the change from its first day with a figure, with 0% dashed; the dashboard's daily-profit bars share one credit scale (a percent change means nothing for profit that crosses zero). `spark()` takes `lo`, `hi` and `base` for this; each caption says the scale.

<a name="Rules-failure-codes"></a>
### Failure codes
What `perform` (and each action word) returns when an action fails. The full list is in the players' manual's table and in [the Lua reference](lua-functions.md).

| Code | Meaning |
|---|---|
| -1 | the other drone isn't at the same place |
| -2 | not enough of a resource aboard (or, for `go`, no Engine) |
| -3 | no room in the hold |
| -4 | not enough credits |
| -5, -6 | the order doesn't allow selling or buying that many |
| -7, -8 | wrong price |
| -9 | the action was stopped |
| -10 | no matching order |
| -12 | refused when performed (from `buy_here`/`sell_here`: nobody here sells or buys it) |
| -14 | no House station here trades that resource that way |
| -15 | no such wreck here, or already plundered |
| -16 | a startup trades only with the House, and plunders nothing |
| -17 | a startup's daily buying cap at this exchange |
| -18 | players, not companies, trade shares under the new rules |
| -19 | an open order can't be withdrawn, cut or worsened in its first 12 game hours |
| -20 | the company already has as many working drones as it may (8) |
| -30 | refused by the drone's fuel floor |

<a name="Rules-numbers"></a>
### Where each number is set
`config/forge.php` reads each `FORGE_*` setting from the environment; `deploy/docker-compose.yml` gives the live server's defaults, which win over the config file's, so change both together (#661). `house_rules` is a one-row table the engines read.

| Rule | Value | Set in |
|---|---|---|
| New rules on | on | `FORGE_NEW_RULES` |
| Accounts verified at once (servers without mail) | off | `FORGE_TRIAL_AUTO_VERIFY` |
| Starting credits for shares | 5,000 | `FORGE_PLAYER_SHARE_CREDITS` |
| Accounts from one address an hour | 3 | `FORGE_PLAYER_SIGNUPS_PER_HOUR` |
| Pupils in a class group | 40 | `FORGE_CLASS_GROUP_MAX` |
| Startups an account | 5 | `FORGE_STARTUP_MAX` |
| Game days a closed startup's slot stays taken | 1 | `forge.startup_slot_days` |
| Guest startups from one address a real day (unused since #871: every startup needs an account) | 5 | `FORGE_STARTUP_GUEST_PER_DAY` |
| A startup's hull, lent | 1,000 | `FORGE_STARTUP_HULL_VALUE` |
| A startup's starting credits, lent | 3,000 | `forge.starting_credits` |
| A startup's fee | 200 | `FORGE_STARTUP_FEE` |
| A push after the first | +100 debt | `FORGE_STARTUP_PUSH_FEE` |
| Credits to mature, above the debt | 500 | `FORGE_STARTUP_MATURE_MARGIN` |
| A startup's credit cap | 2× starting debt (debt less push fees) | `house_rules.startup_credit_cap_permille` (2000) |
| Earth's single modules | at their listed value, no markup: a kit's parts 2,285 to 2,422 against the kit's 2,000 (#514) | `build_game_data.py` (the modules' rows) |
| A startup's daily buying cap | a tenth of what's on sale, at least 5 | `house_rules.startup_buy_permille` (100); the floor is `STARTUP_BUY_FLOOR` in the engine (5, #777) |
| Minimum order life | 12 game hours | `house_rules.order_lock_hours` |
| Working drones a company | 8 | `house_rules.fleet_cap` |
| Shares a matured company | 100 | `PlayerShares::COUNT` |
| Dividends | half of daily profit (a quarter until round 72, #747) | `FORGE_DIVIDEND_PERMILLE` (500) |
| The bank pays after | 30 game days | `FORGE_BANK_PAY_DAYS` |
| Dividends the bank's selling price adds | 10 game days (#781) | `FORGE_BANK_PREMIUM_DAYS` |
| New shares a company offers at a time | 10 (#780) | `PlayerShares::OFFER_UNITS` |
| The most shares a company may have | 200 (#780) | `PlayerShares::MAX_COUNT` |
| Rebate to a resting share order | 5 thousandths (0.5%) of the trade (#782) | `FORGE_REBATE_PERMILLE` |
| A share order rests before it earns one | 3,600 game seconds | `FORGE_REBATE_REST_GAME_SECONDS` |
| Players' trading range | 0.8 to 1.25 × the bank's price | `FORGE_SHARE_RANGE_LOW`, `FORGE_SHARE_RANGE_HIGH` |
| A hull's life, in cash-out | 365 game days | `FORGE_HULL_LIFE_DAYS` |
| A drone counts as stranded after | 3 game days | `forge.stranded_days` |
| The season | a real day, UTC | `PlayerBoards::day()` |
| Sales tax | 5% | `house_rules.sales_tax_permille` (50) |
| Processor tax | 15 a million instructions | `house_rules.compute_tax_per_million` |
| Congestion: the most the processor tax rises | 10× | `house_rules.congestion_cap` |
| Broke days before dissolving | 50 | `house_rules.dissolve_days` |
| Game days without a sale before a matured company is wound up (warned at 30) | 60 | `WindUp::DAYS`, `WindUp::WARN` |
| Mining deposits run dry and refill | off (0) | `house_rules.deposits_deplete` |
| A plunderer's share | 50% | `house_rules.plunder_share` |
| Starting fuel | 100 | `forge.starting_fuel` |

[Return to the TOC](#Table-of-Contents)

## General Mechanics

### All objects are defined by their orbits
Every natural and manmade object in the game will be in orbit around the sun, a planet, or a moon.  Static objects are bodies that are locked in a fixed orbit, which does not change during the game.  Drones are dynamic objects, which can move from one orbit to the next. The Physics Engine routinely updates the cartesian coordinates of static and dynamic objects based on their orbital parameters.

[Return to the TOC](#Table-of-Contents)

### Game time runs on a master clock
Game time can run faster than real time, so that trips and industrial processes measured in days take seconds or minutes to play. One master setting, `GAME_TIME_SCALE` (game seconds per real second, default 1800 since #380 after round 40: a game hour every 2 real seconds, a game day every 48; it was 60, one game hour a real minute), governs everything in the game that takes time:

* **Orbits.** Bodies are placed where they are at the current *game* time.
* **Actions.** Effect times in the action tables are game seconds. An event is scheduled at a game time (`events.game_t` = game now + its delay) and comes due when game time reaches it.
* **Travel.** See below.
* **Signals.** The light-speed delay of commands and observations is the light time in game seconds (see [Information travels at the speed of light](#Information-travels-at-the-speed-of-light)).

The clock is kept in the `game_clock` table so both engines share it: *game time = game_anchor + (real now − real_anchor) × scale*. The Game Engine reads the scale from its configuration (environment variable `GAME_TIME_SCALE`) when it starts. If the scale has changed, it re-anchors the clock at the current game time, so game time, and every orbit, carries on without a jump. Game time starts equal to real time; at any scale above 1 the game's planets run ahead of the real sky. The world's first game second is kept as `game_clock.epoch`, and every change of scale is recorded in `game_clock_history` (real time, game time and the new scale), so charts can map game time to real time.

Everything the engines schedule or compare is stored in game time (game seconds, counted like unix seconds) and compared with the game clock, never with the computer's clock, so it stays right if the scale changes while it waits. Both engines reload the clock every cycle.

<a name="The-Clock-Floor"></a>**The clock floor (#324 step 20).** The clock aims for `GAME_TIME_SCALE` but slows when the engines can't keep up, never below `GAME_TIME_FLOOR` (defaults since #380 after round 40: 1800 and 60, the old live pace; Public Forge ran 24 and 8 before, and `0` means it never floats). A slower clock makes every action take longer in real time, so drones ask for fewer slices and events a real second; computing doesn't slow, but it costs fuel. Once every real minute (`CLOCK_CHECK_SECONDS`) the Game Engine measures how far behind the engines run, in real seconds: the commands that may run now, at the rate it ran them over the minute, or the oldest due event the Event Engine hasn't done, whichever is later (`clock_floor_lag`). Then (`clock_floor_next`, in `clock_floor.c`):

* more than `CLOCK_LAG_HIGH` behind (10 s; 60 until round 40): the scale drops by a quarter, to no less than the floor;
* less than `CLOCK_LAG_LOW` behind (3 s; 15 until round 40): it rises by a quarter, to no more than the target;
* in between: it holds.

Each change re-anchors the clock as above (and is written to `game_clock_history`). Behind by more than `CLOCK_LAG_HIGH` already at the floor, the world is **full** (`game_clock.is_full`), until it is less than `CLOCK_LAG_LOW` behind. The check is recorded in `game_clock` (`target_scale`, `floor_scale`, `lag_seconds`, `slices_per_minute`, `is_full`, `load_checked_at`; migration 0015), and the Home page shows the clock live.

While the world is full, the Release page puts new releases in `launch_queue` instead of launching them, and tells the player their place in line; a waiting release keeps its company name. The scheduler's tick (`/tick`, every few seconds) launches waiting releases in order, at most 5 a time (`LaunchQueue::drain`), while the world isn't full. A load check more than 5 real minutes old (the Game Engine stopped) doesn't count, so releases aren't held on old news.

**Travel time.** Drones travel with constant acceleration, speeding up to the midpoint and slowing down after, so a trip of distance *d* takes *t = 2·√(d ÷ a)* game seconds. *d* is the distance between the two bodies when the drone leaves (at game time), and *a* is `TRAVEL_ACCELERATION` (m/s², default 19.6: 2 g since #380 round 38, when a trip to Mars at 1 m/s² took 10 of a round's 40 game days and no drone that went there profited; trips are now 0.23 as long: Moon about 2.5 game hours, Mars 1.5 to 3.4 days). Every effect of the travel protocol at or after its move (the location effect) is delayed by the trip time, so the engine stays locked until arrival. So drones can plan trips (#380), the Game Engine stores every body's position (`objects.x_coord`, `y_coord`, `z_coord` in km from the Sun, and `computed_on` in game time) and `TRAVEL_ACCELERATION` (`game_clock.travel_acceleration`, migration 0022) once a real minute; queries 703 (where a body is), 704 (a trip from here) and 705 (a trip from A to B) and the `trip` word read them, using the same formula. Before, nothing filled the coordinates and a drone couldn't learn a distance.

Since actions are short and only one runs at a time (see [The Game Engine cycles through player-run virtual machines](#The-Game-Engine-cycles-through-player-run-virtual-machines)), events of one action that land in the same real second at a high scale are still processed in game-time order: the Game Engine inserts an action's events sorted by game time, and the Event Engine processes an action's same-second events in that order. At equal times the action's own fuel cost comes first (`Event_t.burn`: fuel, a fixed amount, from the acting drone; fuel that a push or pull moves is not the cost), then each equipment check (a protocol takes a module out and puts it straight back to show it is aboard) with its two halves together, then the other depletions, then the rest. A failure aborts the rest of the action, so this order means a failure never strands a module out of the hold. One exception (#714): a pull of fuel burns its unit a game second after the last of its effects, once the fuel has arrived, so a drone with an empty tank docked at an exchange can buy fuel (round 70's Juno 69-2 sat at Mars with 0 fuel and 1,780 credits, and every purchase failed on its own fuel cost). A pull of anything else still pays first.

<a name="Aborted-events-settled"></a>**An aborted action's events are settled at once (#725).** When an action was aborted part way, its later events kept outcome 0. The Event Engine's batch query (`outcome = 0`, by `events_pending`, skipping events of aborted actions) walked past every one of them on each batch, and they were never settled. The first deploy database report (`deploy/db-report.sh`) found that query taking 0.9 s and examining 328,000 rows a call, about half of MySQL's time over 10 hours, and it grew with every abort: the round 70 slowdown. `db_abort_action` (in both engines) now marks the action's outcome-0 events -9 when it aborts it, and migration 0070 settled the ones left behind.

<a name="Passing-database-errors"></a>**Passing database errors don't fail actions (#711, #712).** A database error while the Event Engine applies an event used to count as the event failing: the event took that outcome and its action was aborted. When the error fell between a recipe locking its tool and unlocking it (Make Alloys locks the Smelter two game seconds in and unlocks it at the end; mining locks the Worm), the tool stayed locked, and every later make or mine failed with -2. Round 70 found makers with 4 to 7 of their 8 drones dead this way, and a miner whose every mine failed from day 2171, just after the outage of days 2161 to 2170 (a full disk). Now a deadlock (1213), a lock wait timeout (1205), a full disk or table (1021, 1114) or a lost connection (2006, 2013, 2055) marks the batch for a retry (`event_batch_retry`): the rest of the batch is skipped, the Event Engine rolls the batch's transaction back, waits a second and runs it again. Migration 0067 gave back the tools already stuck: each locked holding that no pending event will unlock was moved back to the drone's free stock. Since #748 the Event Engine does the same each time it starts (`db_free_stuck_tools`), so a tool locked by any later outage is freed when the engine comes back: round 71's matured Makers had failed every make for months after the day-2161 outage, and dissolved 400 game days before 0067 ran. Before #380 the fuel burn sat between a check's two halves, and a drone short of fuel lost the module for good. And a pull of fuel put the seller's fuel first: the Event Engine runs a transfer's two halves at once, so the delivery landed before the buyer's 1-fuel cost, which then overwrote it and the buyer never paid.

**Precision.** Game times are whole game seconds, and the engines check for due work every few seconds (`DB_READ_TIME` and `ENGINE_CHECK_TIME`, 5 s by default). At high scales, short actions therefore complete within one engine cycle, and messages can arrive up to a cycle after their light time.

**Where it is stored.**
* `game_clock`: the anchors and the scale.
* `game_clock_history`: every change of scale.
* `events.game_t`: when each event takes place, in game time; `events.timestamp` holds the same moment in real time, for the website. An event inserted without a `game_t` (by the website, such as a new drone's starter kit, or by a script) gets the current game time from the `events_game_t` trigger.
* `observations.game_t`: when a drone (or the Central Database) sees an event, in game time; `observations.time` is the real time, for the website.
* `trades.game_t`: when each trade happened, in game time.
* `commands.deliver_at`: when a player's command reaches its drone, in game time.
* `outbox`: replies on their way to Earth.
* `agents.waiting_action`: the action a drone's script is waiting for.
* `agents.pending_input`: the lines a paused script hasn't read yet.

Examples at the defaults (scale 60, 1 m/s²):

| Trip | Game time | Real time | Light delay (real) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Earth to the Moon | 10.9 hours | 11 minutes | under a second |
| Earth to Mars, closest (0.52 au) | 6.5 days | 2.6 hours | 4 seconds |
| Earth to Mars, farthest (2.67 au) | 14.6 days | 5.9 hours | 22 seconds |
| Earth to Jupiter (5.2 au) | 20 days | 8.2 hours | 43 seconds |
| Earth to Neptune (30 au) | 49 days | 19.6 hours | 4.2 minutes |

[Return to the TOC](#Table-of-Contents)

### Information travels at the speed of light
A drone will not know what is happening at a distance until the light or radio transmission actually reaches it.  This can take minutes to hours depending on the drone’s distance from the observed event. Ex Machinis will handle this phenomena via the tEvents and tObservations tables in the Central Database.

Players are on Earth, so their email to and from drones travels at the speed of light too, scaled by the game clock (see [Game time runs on a master clock](#Game-time-runs-on-a-master-clock)):

* **Player to drone.** When a command is queued (a released script; in the email game, a player's mail), the Game Engine sets `commands.deliver_at` to the game time its signal reaches the drone: game now + the drone's distance from Earth ÷ c. The command runs only after that (meta-commands such as `<abort>` too; engine resumes are immediate).
* **Drone to player.** A reply is written to `drone_messages` with `game_t` set to when the drone's signal reaches Earth, the same light time later; the website shows it from then on. (In the email game it was held in the `outbox` table until then, and emailed.) The reply's first line still gives the drone's distance in light-minutes of game time.
* **Observations.** An event reported to the Central Database is observed the drone's light time from Earth later, in game time (`observations.game_t`).

The Observation Engine is responsible for coordinating the discovery of events by distant drones.
Every time something occurs in the game it will be recorded in the tEvents table along with the location and time of the event.  

The following are examples of recorded events:
* Drone departs or arrives in an orbit.
* Drone starts or stops mining an asteroid.
* The owner emails FORTH commands to a drone.
* The drone emails program output to the owner.

Drones will only be aware of those events that directly affect them, events that are directly observable, or events related to other drones that they are specifically monitoring on their scanners.  The links between drones and observable events will be held in the tObservations table and managed by the Observation Engine.

[Return to the TOC](#Table-of-Contents)

### All ingame events result from actions taken by drones
Drones use onboard resources to perform actions that result in recorded events.  Each action that is performed by a drone will generally:
* Require a particular piece of onboard equipment (an installed resource) to perform;
* Consume a certain quantity of resources at the start of the action (ie. burn fuel, consume minerals, use up ammunition);
* Take a defined amount of time to perform;
* Change a drone’s location and/or inventory when complete.

The Game Engine will respond to specific FORTH commands by recording events associated with the beginning and end of an action in the Events and Observation Tables, respectively.  These events will dictate changes in a drone’s status that occur at the start and stop of the activity.

Examples of some FORTH initiated activities activities that can be performed by drone include:
* Moving to another orbit
* Transferring cargo from one drone to another
* Mining and enriching ores
* Using refined metals to produce components for new drone

[Return to the TOC](#Table-of-Contents)

### Players use FORTH to control their drones

**FORTH was retired after round 38 (#380).** Players now write drones in Lua only: the Release page, `/api/v1/check` and `/release` (and `/test`, until the test world was retired) refuse a FORTH script (`Release::scriptProblem`: "FORTH has been retired"), the starters, company designs, players' manual, word reference (`documents/lua-functions.md`, which replaced `forth-words.md`) and blog are Lua, the House station drones run `resources/house/station.lua`, and the Machine page (a FORTH VM in the browser, `game-engine/machine`, `html/wasm`) and the FORTH dictionary page are gone. The protocol and query descriptions (game data) open with their Lua call, such as `push(N, R, D), or perform(10, N, R, D)` and `ask(620, N, H)`, in place of FORTH's `[N R D 10 perform]` and `[N H R L 620 query]`, and drop FORTH's cells, doubles and counted strings: `build_game_data.py` converts them (`lua_description`), naming the library function for mining, refining, making and the other protocols that have one. The Protocols and Queries pages explain them in Lua. The engine keeps its FORTH VM, so drones released in FORTH before then carry on (`agents.language = 'forth'`). What follows about FORTH describes them, and the engine underneath both languages.

FORTH is a compact, efficient, and very powerful programming language that uses stacks and operator-defined words to organize complex programs.  Each user manages her fleet of drones by defining and applying FORTH words (functions) in drone-specific programs. These words reside in dictionaries belonging to each drone.

In the game, a drone's eForth interpreter is best understood as a **sequencer**: it decides how one in-game action follows another. Every in-game action is started by `perform`, and the script waits while the action takes place. The script only runs in between actions, to look at how the last action went (the value `perform` leaves on the stack), query the drone's surroundings and decide what to do next. A drone therefore performs one action at a time, and the time a script spends computing is negligible next to the game time its actions take.

Generally, a user will test out new routines on a single drone before uploading them to other drones in his fleet.
Programs are usually tested on drone operating nearby to reduce the delay associated with messages traveling long distances through space.

[Return to the TOC](#Table-of-Contents)

### Players advance by developing more complex codes
A new player starts by sending simple real-time commands to drone that are operating in his vicinity.  However, as the player expands his operation in search of resources and opportunities, the commands necessarily become more complex.  This complexity results from the challenges associated with:
* The demands of simultaneously operating multiple drones.
* Communication delays associated with drone operating light minutes away from the player.
* The need to have drones carry-on activities when the player is not online.

FORTH is well suited for this sort of progressive engagement because the player develops and manages an increasingly complex dictionary of words that she can can use to command her drones. These commands can be very simple at first, requiring constant user intervention to pilot the drone. However, the drone’s vocabulary can quickly expand to include more complex commands as the player becomes a sophisticated operator. In theory, there are no limits to the amount of autonomy and complex decision making the player can off-load onto her drone.

[Return to the TOC](#Table-of-Contents)

<a name="Companies-are-experiments"></a>
### Companies are experiments; the market, not the rules, rescues drones
In Public Forge a released company runs on its own, and every company eventually dies of something its program didn't foresee: a market that moves, a dry deposit, a rival, a coding flaw. That is the game, not a failure of it. Players advance by iterating: they read a company's reports, trades and post-mortem, fix or rethink the program, and release the next version as a new company. The manuals should say so plainly, and the game should reward a lineage of better programs over keeping one flawed company alive.

<a name="Every-Release-Is-Live"></a>**Every release is live (#380, after round 40).** There is no test world any more ([retired](#The-Test-World)): the game world is the one big test environment. Releases are free and unlimited apart from the API's rate limit (`api-write`, 10 a minute per address), and a released drone still can't be changed, so a player iterates by releasing a script, watching its drone (its page, `/api/v1/drones/{id}`, the `script_status` line it sets with `set_status`, its messages and timeline) and releasing a better version as a new company; failing costs nothing but the lesson. To make that loop quick, the live clock runs at the old test world's pace: `GAME_TIME_SCALE` 1800 (a game day every 48 real seconds, 40 game days in about 32 real minutes), floating down when the engines fall behind (`CLOCK_LAG_HIGH` 10 real seconds, `CLOCK_LAG_LOW` 3) to no less than `GAME_TIME_FLOOR` 60, the old live pace, where the world is full and releases queue ([The clock floor](#The-Clock-Floor)). `/api/v1/clock` gives the pace now (`scale`, `real_seconds_per_game_day`) beside the target (`target_scale`, `target_real_seconds_per_game_day`), the floor and a `slowed` flag (#418: round 40's newcomers took a pace they read once for the usual one). The manuals and the blog say "release early, release often".

So the rules add **no safety nets** for drones that get themselves into trouble (#380):
* A drone that runs out of fuel is a wreck. There is no free fuel, no free order and no rescue; buying fuel costs fuel like any trade (round 23's free ordering from the House and a brief free pull of fuel were both taken out).
* Trouble is a market opportunity instead. A drone running low posts a buy order for fuel at a price that pays a seller to come to it, and waits (waiting costs nothing). Fuel couriers, drones that carry fuel to where others run low and push it into those orders, are a business the market makes possible. The same goes for anything else a stranded drone needs.
* The House's station drones are market participants like any other: no special trade path, only their Import and Export modules (see [House station drones](#Station-Drones)). Nor do players get House-specific information: no price list (`house-price` and query 633 are gone), no House price board (681), no station table on the Market page, no station markers on the Map, and query 630 answers only the station drones themselves. The one exception (#380 round 36): the news (`/api/v1/news`) names the booms, slumps, shortages and gluts under way, by exchange and tier, not their prices, as a market's news would. Players learn the House's prices from its orders and trades, like anyone's; the leaderboard values goods at market prices. Since #550 (round 58), the House's **demand** is public, as a real market publishes its capacity: how much more each exchange buys today, its bid now and when it refills (`HouseMarket::demand`, query 706, `house_demand(r)`, `/api/v1/house-demand`, the Market page). Since #630 (round 64), its **supply** is public too: what each exchange has on sale, what it refills to and when its next round of restocking is due (`HouseMarket::supply`, query 707, `house_supply(r)`, `/api/v1/house-supply`, the Market page). Its listed prices are still learned from the market.
* When a change is proposed to save drones from a common mistake, prefer documenting the market solution, a word in the example library or a better starter script over a new mechanic.

[Return to the TOC](#Table-of-Contents)

**Lua drones (#380).** A drone's program may be Lua 5.4 instead of FORTH (round 34's newcomers all chose Lua; `game-engine/lua-poc/` has the measurements behind it). `agents.language` is `forth` or `lua` (migration 0027); `Release::isLua` sets it when a script defines a function `launched` and no FORTH word `launched`. Since FORTH was retired (above) every new drone is Lua.

* **Loading.** The release splits a Lua script into pieces at line ends, as it does a FORTH one, and the engine appends each to `agents.lua_script` (no FORTH line re-wrapping: `engine_apply_max_cycle_lines` is skipped). On `<launched>` (`engine_process_meta_launched`), `lua_drone.c` builds the drone: a `lua_State` with the base library (less `load`, `loadfile`, `dofile`, `require`, `collectgarbage`), `string`, `table`, `math` (less `randomseed`) and `utf8`; the standard library (`lua_library.lua`, made into `lua_library.c` by the Makefile); the script's top level; its saved variables; then `launched` in a coroutine. `release_vm` is set to `''`, so the drone counts as loaded (normal slices, no loading priority).
* **Running.** The drone's Lua stays in the engine's memory between slices (`lua_drone.c` keeps them by agent ID); no VM image is loaded or saved. A count hook charges each Lua instruction as `LUA_INSTRUCTION_WEIGHT` (6) of the FORTH VM's to `vm_meter` (the Systems shop's scoring took 374 Lua instructions a round against 2,149), so slices, fuel for thinking and the instructions shown are as for FORTH, and ends the slice by yielding when the budget is used (`vm_slice_yield`: the engine queues the resume as for FORTH). Lua can't yield inside a function called from C (a `table.sort` comparator, a `string.gsub` replacement): round 55 (#380) wrecked two drones with "attempt to yield across a C-call boundary" (#508). There the hook lets the script run on (`vm_meter_hook` decides, `lua_isyieldable` tells it where it is), and it yields at its first hook back in its own code; past `LUA_UNPAUSABLE_INSTRUCTIONS` (2,000,000) run on, it raises an error, so a loop inside a comparator can't hold the engine. A drone may hold `LUA_MEMORY_CAP` (256 KB): its own allocator refuses past it and Lua raises "not enough memory".
* **perform and query.** `perform(protocol, ...)` calls `protocol_execute` with its arguments on a stack of cells instead of the FORTH VM's (`VmExtension_t.cells`: `vm_extension_pop`, `_push`, `_depth` and `_read_counted` use them when set), a double parameter (`double_params`) split into its two cells and a string (a broadcast) passed by index; an accepted action yields, and the resume hands `perform` its result (`engine_finish_perform` -> `lua_drone_set_result`). `query(id, ...)` fills the query's tags from its arguments (the last is `[value_1]`; a `[dvalue_k]` is one argument) and returns the rows as tables, by position and by column name (`d_` dropped).
* **Saving and restarting.** Whenever the drone pauses for an action, its plain-data globals (numbers, strings, booleans, tables of them; not the library's, but for the library's own state: `mode`, `ticks_in_mode`, `my_orders`, `my_orders_of`, `LIBRARY_STATE` in `lua_drone.c`, since round 38, when a restarted drone was back in mode 0 and posted its orders again) are saved to `agents.lua_state` as Lua source. A clone (`db_e.c`'s launch) gets the parent's `language`, `lua_script` and `lua_state`, as a FORTH clone gets its VM. If the engine restarts, a Lua drone's next command rebuilds it from its script and saved variables and runs `launched` again from the top (a FORTH drone carries on in its saved VM); a crash restarts it with no saved variables (`lua_drone_forget_state`), as a FORTH drone restarts from `release_vm`. Scripts must therefore let `launched` run again: `await_kit` and `unpack` pass once the kit is unpacked. `equip` (#726) treats a query that failed as "can't tell" and asks again a game minute later, and buys a kit only when a tool is still missing a game minute after it first looked: `units()` reads a failed query as 0, and the Engine is out of the hold for a game second at each end of a flight (Activate Engine takes it from the free stock and puts it back locked, then the reverse). `test_lua_equip.c` (`make test`) runs `equip` against a pretend drone. A reply's **Location** line (`engine_send_output`) reads the drone's place when it is sent (`db_drone_place`: its last move event due by now, applied or not, else `agents.object_id`): the events engine runs a few game seconds behind, and a drone restarted a game second after reaching Earth said "Location: Mars".
* **Showing code (#848).** Wherever the website shows a script (a company's code and its revisions, the ```` ```lua ```` blocks of the players' manual and the blog), `LuaHighlight` colours it: each token escaped in a span by kind (keyword, literal, string, number, comment, operator, Lua's own name, the game library's name, a function's own name), coloured by `pre.lua` in `_components.scss`. The text is the script unchanged, so code that doesn't parse still shows as written; the block is `v-pre`, so Lua's `{{` isn't read by Vue (#592). Unlabelled Markdown blocks (a drone's output) stay plain.
* **Typing code (#852).** Every box where a player types Lua is a `<textarea data-lua-editor>` (with `@include('partials.lua-editor')` beside it, which puts the game's names for the colours on the page once). `resources/assets/js/lua-editor.js`, loaded by `app.js` as its own chunk only on pages that have such a box, turns each into a CodeMirror 6 editor: line numbers, bracket matching, auto-indent, undo, and Lua read by the legacy Lua mode (strings, comments, indents) with names, numbers and operators read as `LuaHighlight` reads them, so the colours are the same `pre.lua` classes. The textarea stays the form's field: hidden (`.lua-editor-source`), given the editor's text on every change with an `input` event; a script that sets its value fires `input` on it to update the editor (the starter cards do). Its `maxlength` holds in the editor, a `required` empty box still stops the form and focuses the editor, and its label focuses the editor. Phones get 16px text (no zoom on focus) and wrapped lines. If the chunk never loads, the plain textarea is still there and works. The textareas are `v-pre`, so Lua's `{{` isn't read by Vue.
* **Checking.** `bin/lua-check` (built with the engine; the website's image builds its own) compiles a script without running it; `Release::luaProblem` runs it (`forge.lua_check`) so `/check`, the Release page and the API report a syntax error with its line. Without it, the drone reports the error when it loads.

<a name="Asking"></a>**Asking about the world by name (#380, after round 39; Lua library 19).** Round 39's newcomers wanted quotes from other exchanges, stock levels, the cost of a whole lot, their own profit and loss, and a way for a drone to say why it was idle. The Lua library (`lua_library.lua`) has a named function for every query worth asking (about 50: `hold()`, `sell_orders(r)`, `buy_orders(r)`, `trip_to(p)`, `price_board(r)`, `deposits()`, `wrecks()`, `lineage()`, `event(id)`...), each returning plain values or records with named fields (`records(rows, names)` maps a row's columns, so no query's SQL changed); one about a drone or place asks about this one when the argument is left out. Each query's description on the Queries page opens with its function (`QUERY_FUNCS` in `build_game_data.py`). New queries:
* **615, orders everywhere** (`orders_everywhere(r)`; `quotes(r)` and `stock(r, place)` reduce it in Lua): every exchange's orders for a resource, live where the drone is and as they stood an hour of game time ago elsewhere (the price board's `BOARD_LAG`), so the knowledge rules hold. Orders and holdings are events, so the past is rebuilt: each drone's latest order event by then (`l`, by drone and side) and what it held by then (`holding_asof`: for each installed/locked kind, the latest inventory event by then, found walking back from the newest with `ORDER BY id DESC LIMIT 1`; a NOT EXISTS like `holding()`'s with a time limit took 1.1 s over a station's thousands of events, this takes about 0.1 s, about a third of query 610's time). Drones flying, wrecked, or arrived after then are left out, and buyers who can't pay for a unit.
* **655, my company's trades**: the trades table over the last H game hours, by resource, where the drone's company sold or bought (`earnings(hours)`: sales, taxes, purchases, net, by resource).
* **845, event details**: an event in one row (810 to 844 ask a column each), with its game day and seconds from `game_clock.epoch` (843's description promised those; its SQL still returns `FROM_UNIXTIME`).
* **`set_status(text)`**, a Lua built-in (`l_set_status`, `db_set_agent_status`): up to 80 characters (a longer line cut at its last space before the 77th and ended "...", `status_cut`, #715: the Trader starter's line was cut at "release th"), control characters made spaces, into `agents.script_status` and `script_status_t` (migration 0030), for the price of a query. The drone page shows it ("Its script says"), `GET /drones/{id}` and company drone lists give `script_status` `{text, set, set_game_time}`, and test results give it with the drone's `place`.
* `cost_to_buy(n, r)` and `sale_value(n, r)` walk `sell_orders` and `buy_orders` in Lua (no new query).
* The engine copies a query's SQL and a protocol's or query's description with `snprintf` into their fixed buffers (2,000 and 255 bytes): `sprintf` wrote one byte past a 255-character description, onto the protocol's `process_multiplier` (#411: launches ran zero times).
* Three example companies defined a global `stock` or `hold`, now library names: they are `stock_up`, `goods_held` and `held`.

### The Game Engine cycles through player-run virtual machines
Since there will be a rather large number of drones running FORTH programs at any given time, the Game Engine (GE) cycles through their virtual machines. Work for the GE is queued in the `commands` table: a player's emailed script, or a "resume" entry for a script that has paused. Each cycle (every `DB_READ_TIME` seconds, 5 by default) the GE takes the oldest command it may run, loads that drone's VM from `agents.vm`, runs it, and saves the VM again. The VM holds everything needed to carry on later: the stacks, the dictionary, variables and where the script stopped.

A drone is always in one of these states:
* **Idle:** no script is running. A new script from the player starts at once.
* **Running:** a script has used up its slice of instructions (`MAX_CYCLE_INSTRUCTIONS`, 100,000 by default) and paused. The GE queues a resume and continues it on a later cycle, so long scripts don't hold up other drones. The VM reads a script one line at a time; when it pauses (here or while waiting, below), the lines it hasn't read yet are kept in `agents.pending_input` and fed back to it when it resumes, so the script carries on with its next line. What the script prints is collected (`agents.output`) and emailed in one message when it ends.
