* **Waiting:** the script called `perform` and the action it started has not finished. The script stays paused, with `agents.waiting_action` set to the action's ID, until every event of that action has been processed by the Event Engine (or the action is aborted). Only then is the drone's resume taken from the queue: `perform` leaves its result on the stack (see [Returned Value](#Returned-Value)) and the script carries on. The GE doesn't load a waiting drone's VM to check on it; the queue simply skips its resume until the action is done.

So a drone performs one in-game action at a time, and its script always knows how the last one went before deciding what to do next. While a drone is running or waiting, a new script waits its turn: the next-command query holds it until the drone has no resume queued (#367; it used to be refused with "Another command is running, retry later", which dropped the rest of any released script longer than one 2,048-character command). `<abort>` and the other meta commands are never held (see [Meta Commands](#Meta-Commands)).

<a name="Engine-Throughput"></a>**Throughput (#324 step 20).** The load generator (`php artisan forge:load-test N --database=...`, never the live database) releases N drones running the starters in turn, through the same path as the Release page, and `--report` shows how the engines keep up. It found four limits, now gone:

* The Game Engine slept `DB_READ_TIME` (5 s) after every command, so it ran one slice per 5 seconds. It now sleeps only when there was nothing to run.
* Loading a drone's saved VM first set up a fresh one (callback words, word library, game words: millions of instructions) and then overwrote all of it with the saved core. It now just loads the core.
* The Event Engine did one batch of `OUTCOME_EVENT_BATCH_SIZE` (10) events per `ENGINE_CHECK_TIME` (5 s). It now runs the next batch at once while batches come back full. Since #432 a batch is 100 events. Each batch's query sorts every due event, so with 1,000 ships batches of 10 made that query the Event Engine's biggest cost (8.6 ms a batch). Each event still checks, just before it is processed, that its action wasn't aborted.
* The Game Engine found each command to run with a query over every due command (`db_get_next_command`), once per command. With 1,000 ships that cost about 9 ms a turn, and 70 ms while a thousand releases loaded (#432). It now fetches up to 64 due commands at a time in run order, and checks each again by id, with the same rules, just before it runs it. A command that stopped being runnable meanwhile is skipped. The batch is refilled when it runs out or is a real second old, so a new release still loads first within about a second. Measured on a settled 1,000-ship world (the same saved state, 5 real minutes each):

| | Before #432 | Command batches | Command batches and 100-event batches |
|---|---|---|---|
| Events settled | 59,008 | 60,217 | **73,134** |
| Game hours passed | 92 | 92 | **113** |
| Clock at the end | 427× | 427× | **759×** |
| Engines behind at the end | 10 s | 15 s | **9 s** |

* The command batches took the Game Engine's top query (138 s of MySQL time in 5 minutes) off the list, but on their own changed little, because the Event Engine was the limit.
* With 100-event batches, its due-events query dropped off the list too.
* The top costs left are per event: the company's latest event (#435), the drone's inventory row, and each event's update.

Each run restarts the engines on a saved world, so a backlog makes the clock step down at first. It only speeds up again once the engines are under 3 s behind, so where the clock ends after 5 minutes understates the gain.
* Every observable event got one row in `observations` per drone where it happened, so a thousand drones in one place meant a thousand inserts an event, and the table grew as events × drones (12 million rows in 12 real minutes). Now nothing is stored per drone for it: see [Local sightings](#Local-Sightings).
* **Nor for a drone's own events (#814).** Every settled event also got an observation row for the drone it affected: half the `observations` table, and a write on every event. Query 803 now finds a drone's own events by `events.drone`, so `observations` holds only the Earth database's rows (drone 0, for reportable actions). Migration 0081 also dropped `id_UNIQUE` on `events`, `events_archive` and `observations`, which only repeated the primary key.
* **A tool in use is a lock, not four events (#814).** Each mine, refine and make wrote four to six inventory events for its tool: a check (+1, −1), the lock (free −1, locked +1) and the reverse at the end. That was a third of the game data's resource effects (1,422 became 942). Now:
  * **Two events, no history.** The game data (`tool_locks()` in `build_game_data.py`) gives each a lock tool event (15) at the first of those times and an unlock tool event (16) at the last. The same resource used up as an input stays an input (craft 6121 uses a 5521 and locks another).
  * **Settling.** The events engine settles lock tool against the drone's free holding less its locks in force (`tool_locks`, migration 0082): it fails −2 as a missing tool did, or adds the lock. Unlock tool removes it, and an aborted action's locks go with the abort (the #748 class of stuck tools, at the source).
  * **No sale of a tool in use.** A decrement of a free holding fails −2 if it would leave fewer units than its locks. The events engine keeps the locks in memory, so this costs no query unless it would refuse.
  * **Not history.** Settled lock and unlock events are deleted by the archiver's pass, not archived. Movable stock (query and API order books) subtracts the locks, and recipes read the tool from the lock effect.
  * **Deploys mid-action.** Actions under way at a deploy keep their old four-event locks, which still settle.

**Measured** (one 4-core sandbox, MySQL in Docker, 1,000 starter drones): before the fixes, 12 slices a real minute; after them, about 4,200 slices and 4,500 events a real minute in steady play, with the engines 4 to 9 real seconds behind at a 600× clock (the floor's governor dipped to 337× while all 1,000 drones arrived at the Moon at once, and was back at 600× within three minutes). So Public Forge's target of 24× has room to spare at this size. One cost grows with the world and is left for later: every drone's 64 KB VM is read and written each slice, which is the main cost left as the world grows.

**Aging worlds (#380).** A world slows down as its `events` table grows, because two queries read every event. Both have indexes since migration 0020:
* `sqInventory` (what a drone holds: the `units` game word, query 212, the House's assessments) finds each drone's latest inventory event per resource, installed and locked state. Index `events_inventory` (drone, resource, installed, locked, outcome, event_type) lets MySQL read one index entry per group. In a 90-day test world with 440,000 events, a call fell from about 136 ms to 16 ms, and MySQL's load from two cores to under one. The game-data queries' holdings (`holding()` and `holding_asof()` in `build_game_data.py`; #422) use the same index one drone at a time. For each kind (installed, locked) they take the newer of the latest increment (`MAX(id)` with `event_type = 1`) and the latest depletion (`event_type = 2`). Each is a single step down the index, so a holding costs the same however long the game has run. Before, a `NOT EXISTS` for a later event, and then an `ORDER BY id` over both types, read the drone's whole history for the resource. With 183 drones and 510,000 events (round 41, day 70) an order book took about 160 ms, and the clock fell to a tenth of 1,800×. Now it takes about 8 ms, and the same world held 1,800× with about 1,450 turns a minute.
* The Event Engine's "next due events" (outcome 0, by game time) read and sorted every event; index `events_pending` (outcome, game_t) reads only the pending ones.
* Each inventory or order event looked up its company's latest event (`PREVIOUS_EVENT_BY_OWNER`, for `event_update_new_credit`), though only an event that carries credits uses it (#435). About three in four events carry none: depletions and most increments. On round 41's aged world the lookup was the top cost, about 11 ms each, because MySQL may walk the whole event history backwards to find an idle drone's last event. Events without credits now skip it. Over 5 real minutes on the same saved state, drone turns rose from about 800 a minute to about 1,450, and the engines ran 4 to 5 s behind instead of 6 to 10 s.
* **Lock waits (#439).** Inserting events spent 127 of its 133 s waiting for locks: the events engine held a shared lock on the end of `events` until its batch committed.
  * The fuel refund after a failed transfer was an `INSERT ... SELECT` whose subqueries read `events`. Inside an `INSERT` those are locking reads, and the cargo lookup walks back from the newest row. It now reads first, then inserts plain values.
  * The current-state triggers (migration 0032) no longer read `events` for a new or settled event. They keep whichever row has the larger id. Only an event that stops counting is recomputed from the history.
  * Inserts then took 7.7 s in 5 minutes, with no lock wait, and drone turns rose from about 1,450 a minute to about 2,100.
* **Current state in the engines (#431, step 1).** Two per-turn questions read the current state instead of the history:
  * When a new action may start (`db_get_latest_processed_game_t`): "not failed" is just outcomes 0 and 1, so it takes the newer of the drone's latest pending and latest done event, each one step back down `events_drone_game_t`. It used to take `MAX` over every event the drone ever had: about 8 ms a call, about 40 ms for a station drone.
  * A drone's inventory, in the events engine, now comes from `holdings`.

  Both gave the same answers as before for every drone of round 41's world. Drone turns rose from about 2,100 a minute to about 2,800–2,960.
* **Current state in the engines (#431, steps 2 and 3).**
  * The previous event of a holding (`PREVIOUS_EVENT_BY_RESOURCE_INFO`, for every inventory event settled) read every such event the drone had and sorted them by game time: 566 for a station drone's fuel. Migration 0033 adds `events_inventory_time` (drone, resource, installed, locked, outcome, game_t), and the engine names it, so it is one step back. That took 2.6 ms down to 0.04 ms, with the same row.
  * The game engine saves a drone's input (`agents.input`, left from the email era) only when it changed. Most turns resume with the same command, and 15 of the 19 s these updates took was waiting for the row's lock.
* **A plan that stalled the engine (#442).** For each seller, query 615 works out what it held an hour ago. With some sampled statistics, MySQL planned that as a reverse scan of all of `events` (`ORDER BY id DESC` by PRIMARY). That took about 25 s a call, and the game engine stalled in two load-test runs. The query now names `events_inventory`: 0.16 s on the same statistics. Two runs after the fix held 1,800× at about 2,900–3,200 turns a minute.
* **Static game data in memory (#431).** The game engine now keeps query definitions, resources and event types in memory (`static_cache.c`).
  * Entries expire after 60 real seconds, so game data reloaded by a deploy reaches running engines.
  * For queries, only the table's fields are cached, never the caller's parameters or results.
  * The purge of month-old actions ran after every successful event; it now runs at most once a real minute.
  * The `INSERT IGNORE` of the clock row ran every engine loop; it now runs only until the clock has loaded.
  * MySQL now answers about 14% fewer statements a second for the same work (5,400 against 6,300). Drone turns stayed at about 3,000 a minute, because each engine waits on its round trips one at a time. The saving is headroom for running drones on several cores (#433).
* **Shared answers about the world (#694).** In a load world of 453 drones, the game engine spent three quarters of its time waiting on MySQL, and drones' Lua queries were 59% of that: the same order books, deposits and price board asked for many times a second by drones at one place.
  * The game engine keeps the result of these queries for `LUA_QUERY_CACHE_MS` real milliseconds (500; 0: off) and gives it to every drone the answer is the same for (`query_cache.c`, `l_query` in `lua_drone.c`): local sell and buy orders (610, 600), every exchange's orders (615) and the deposits (640) by place; the price board (680) and the House's supply (707) for everyone; a company's health (699) for everyone, 10 seconds (#725: 104,000 calls at 69 ms each over 10 hours); who owns a drone (201) for a minute; a drone's own ID (205) and whether it is a startup (708, two seconds) for that drone.
  * The key is the query's SQL with its parameters, before the drone's ID and the game time are put in, plus the place or drone. A drone's questions about itself (its hold, its credits, its orders) are never shared.
  * At 1,800× half a real second is 15 game minutes, so a drone may see an order that has just gone: the trade fails (-10, or a price code) and costs no fuel. Each game engine keeps its own cache. A drone is still charged the query's instructions.
* **Short transactions (#431).** A drone's turn was one transaction, from fetching its command to saving its machine, so every row an action wrote stayed locked until the turn ended.
  * Now `perform` and the fuel a drone's thinking burns each commit the turn's work so far, run, and commit their own action and events at once (`protocol_execute`, `protocol_burn_power`).
  * Locks last as long as one action. The database holds an action as soon as the drone's script counts on it; a Lua drone's script lives in memory, which a rollback never undid.
  * A turn that fails after an action keeps the action.
  * On round 41's world one engine ran as fast as before, about 3,000 turns a minute, and the history checks held.
* **Several game engines (#433), tried and shelved.** Each engine took the drones whose ID left its remainder. Per-drone MySQL named locks kept events in game-time order.
  * With whole-turn transactions, three engines were slower than one, because they deadlocked across named and row locks on the House's station drones.
  * With short transactions the deadlocks went. Even so, on a settled 1,000-ship world three engines did no better than one: about 6,600 turns a minute against about 5,000–6,500, and the clock fell to 1,012× against 1,350×. The events engine, which settles every action alone, was the limit at about 15,000 events a minute.
  * Three engines also left 17 inventory entries whose newest event by id wasn't the newest by game time.
  * The events engine's throughput comes first.
* **Several game engines, revisited (#433).** With the events engine and the game engine's round trips cut (#447 to #460), the shelved design went back in. `ENGINE_SHARDS` game engines each run the drones whose ID leaves their `ENGINE_SHARD` as remainder. Shard 0 also does the shared work: stamping commands, the outbox, positions, and the clock's pace from every shard's turns (`engine_shards`, migration 0036). Only shard 0 sets the scale, so the others read the clock afresh. Per-drone MySQL named locks, taken in drone ID order from scheduling an action's events to the commit, keep events in game-time order across engines; with several engines each session reads committed data. One engine (the default) takes no locks and runs as before.
  * On the saved 3,000-ship world, on a 4-core machine, 3 engines ran 23,000–27,000 drone turns a minute against about 9,800 for one, and the clock held 1,800× for three and a half minutes. Then the events engine fell behind (about 18,000 events a minute, 10,000 due events waiting by the fifth minute), and the clock slowed to 1,012×. Over 5 minutes the game advanced 144 hours against 92.
  * Holdings, orders and each order's stock matched the history. 2 inventory kinds were out of time order (one engine shows 0 to 29 on the same check), and no drone crashed, wrecked or paid power ahead of its instructions.
  * The events engine is the limit again.
* **Each order carries its place (#462, migration 0037).** With three game engines, MySQL used about 3.1 of the test machine's 4 cores, and the events engine fell behind waiting on it rather than on its own work. The local order books were a third of MySQL's time: each read the open orders for the resource and looked up every seller's place. `latest_orders.place` is now the drone's `agents.object_id`, set when the order is recorded and kept by a trigger on `agents` when the drone's place changes (a move settling). A drone in transit keeps the place it left until it arrives, as `agents` does, and the books still leave out drones with a move to come. A local book reads only the open orders here, in price order (`latest_orders_here`): 0.4 ms against about 1.5 ms measured alone. Under full load every statement waits for CPU, so a book still took about 2.5 ms. 420 sampled books gave the same rows in the same order as before, and every order's place matched its drone after a run.
  * On 4 cores, 2 engines kept events current and held the clock at 1,350× for 5 minutes, advancing 121 game hours; 3 engines advanced 144 but fell behind after three and a half minutes. The machine, not the events engine's code, is the limit there.
* **Fewer round trips per settled event (#446).** On the settled 1,000-ship world, the events engine's statements were measured one by one. It now skips those whose answer it already has:
  * It no longer looks up the drone's previous event. Only a log line used it: the cargo comes from `holdings`. It was the engine's top cost (30 s of each 5 minutes).
  * It reads an event's action once per event, not two or three times.
  * A protocol's observable and reportable flags, and the bodies of the solar system (the Sun, the Earth and each orbit up to the Sun), come from `static_cache.c`. Each reported event read them all from MySQL.
  * Whether a batch has already settled an event (the second half of a transfer) comes from a list it keeps. Only the events engine settles events, and a batch is one transaction, so the list says what MySQL said.
  * The delete of a kind's expired history (days old) runs at most once a real hour per kind, not after every successful event.
  * Over 5 real minutes on the same saved state, the clock held 1,800× throughout, and the game advanced 151 hours. Before, it fell to 1,350× after two minutes, and the game advanced 129 hours. Due events waiting to settle stayed in the hundreds, against 2,000–4,000 before. Holdings and orders matched the history, and no more inventory kinds were out of time order than in the saved state.
* **Observations and places once per batch (#448).** A settled event's observations and its place (where drones there see it) were each a round trip. They are now held for the batch and written just before it commits: observations as one multi-row insert, places as one `UPDATE events ... JOIN agents`.
  * Places waiting to be written are written before a move settles, so an event still records where its drone was. A fuel refund writes them before it reads the burn's place.
  * A transfer rolled back to its savepoint drops what it added.
  * On the 1,000-ship world, about 145,000 observation inserts and 48,000 place updates every 5 minutes became about 1,200 and 4,600. The events engine's CPU time fell from 36 s to 30 s. The clock held 1,800× as before, and holdings and orders matched the history. The share of settled events without a place (those no other drone observes) was the same as before.
* **Flying drones in the order books (#450).** The local order books (600, 601, 610, 611, 612) and 615 leave out drones on a trip: those with a pending move still to come. With some table statistics, MySQL planned that check as a scan of the whole of `events` on every call: 78 ms for one order book on a 1,000-ship world's 300,000 events, and growing with the history. The queries now name `events_pending` (outcome, game_t), which holds only the events still to come: about 1.7 ms for the same book, with the same rows. The benchmark's plan had not taken the scan, so its throughput was unchanged. What remains of an order book's cost, about 2.7 ms, is reading each order for the resource and its seller's stock.
* **Open orders indexed (#452, migration 0034).** On a 3,000-ship world, the clock fell from 1,800× to 427× within 5 minutes. The game engine was the limit, and the sell order books took 126 s of the 300: 5.3 ms a call. Each book read all ~1,776 sell orders for the resource, then each seller's place and stock, and returned almost nothing: nearly every drone lists a sell order, and almost none holds more than its kept minimum (579 of 8,250 orders were open). Now `latest_orders` carries the stock and whether the order is open, and a book reads only the open orders: 0.6 ms a call, 22 s of the 300. Drone turns went from about 3,900 to 7,500 a minute on the same saved world. 288 sampled books gave the same rows in the same order as before, and every order's stock matched its holdings after the run. The clock still slowed, with the engines 15–26 s behind against about 40: the next limit is the game engine's other round trips.
* **The command batch (#454, migration 0035).** The game engine fills its batch of 64 due commands about once a real second. One query checked every due command, about one per drone, then sorted them all, so that drones whose script is still loading go first: 25 ms a batch on the 3,000-ship world (1.7 s cold). Now two queries each stop at the batch size: the loading drones' commands, found by an index on `agents` (`release_vm IS NULL`), then the rest, oldest first. Same run order, about 0.7 ms a batch. Drone turns went from about 7,500 to 8,000 a minute.
* **The House's station drones go first (#633, migration 0054).** A third query, before the other two, takes the commands of drones with a `station_id` (found by the `agents_station` index). House supply (#630) showed why: behind 695 player drones on a slowed clock, each station turn came game hours late, so `station.lua`'s hourly rounds of restocking came about every 8 game hours and Earth and the Moon sold out of Metals (A1) for two game days. The House is infrastructure, not a competitor: a handful of drones waiting a game hour between rounds, so this costs players little.
* **Fewer station actions a round (#900, after #835).** Each of a station's actions ends on its next turn, about a game hour later, so a round of up to 90 imports took 2.5 game days at Earth and 7.5 at Mars, and a sold-out good waited days. `station.lua` now tops up its fuel only once it is down to half of what it has for sale (it did after every import, which doubled a round), restocks the emptiest goods first, and ships a bought good down only once there is a quarter of a day's demand of it to send. `HouseMarket::restocks()` marks rounds by the program's wait between them, over 10 game days (#835: it took any import half a game hour after the last as a new round, and said rounds came every game hour).
* **Protocols, location effects and the clock held (#456).** For every turn and action, the game engine asked MySQL for the protocol's row, its location effects and the clock: about 234,000 statements in 5 minutes on the 3,000-ship world. Protocols and location effects now come from `static_cache.c` (60 s, so a deploy's game data is picked up). The game engine reuses a clock read made within the same real second: it is the only program that sets the scale, and setting it updates what it holds. The events engine still reads the clock afresh. Those statements fell to about 1,200, and drone turns went from about 8,000 to 8,500–8,800 a minute.
* **What the engine already knows (#431 step 1).** Three more per-turn round trips went:
  * A drone that pauses queues a resume command. It was inserted unstamped, found by the stamping pass and stamped "due now" (resumes have no light delay): now it goes in already stamped.
  * The stamping pass (commands from players, which travel at light speed) and the check for replies due at Earth run once a real second, not every turn.
  * Resource and market effects, like location effects, come from `static_cache.c`.
  * On the 3,000-ship world, drone turns went from about 8,600 to 9,700 a minute, and the clock held 570× instead of falling to 427×.
* **The drone's own row, once a turn (#431 step 2).** A turn read the drone's `agents` row six or seven times, a column or two at a time: whether its launch is running, crashes in a row, wrecked, still loading, instructions, power paid, the action it waits for, its unread input. Now one statement loads them at the start of the turn. The engine's functions that write those columns keep the copy up to date, and write nothing when a value is already what it would set. The drone's place, which the events engine changes, is still read from MySQL. On the 3,000-ship world, statements per turn went from about 43 to 37, and drone turns from about 9,700 to 9,700–10,400 a minute. Holdings and orders matched the history, and no power bill ran ahead of the instructions used.
* **The turn's writes, once (#431 step 3).** The waiting action (cleared by a resume, set by a perform), the unread input, the instructions run and the power paid are held for the turn and written in one statement before it commits, instead of three or four. The drone's Lua variables are saved only when they changed (by a hash of the last save), though most turns change them. Nothing reads these columns during a turn except the game engine itself. On the 3,000-ship world, statements per turn went from about 37 to 35. Drone turns stayed about the same (9,700–10,300 a minute; runs vary by about 5%). The game engine's connection spends about two thirds of its time waiting on MySQL, about 4 ms a turn over some 29 statements.

<a name="Current-State"></a>**Current state beside the history (#430, migration 0031).** Two tables say what the history says, so a look is one row instead of a pass over `events`:
* `holdings` (drone, resource, installed, locked → units, event_id): each kind's newest successful increment or depletion. An event with no installed or locked flag is kept under -1, so `sqInventory` counts it as it always did, and `holding()` reads only 0 and 1, as before. `sqInventory` is now a sum over `holdings`.
* `latest_orders` (drone, resource, event_type 4 or 5 → price, quantity, event_id): each drone's newest successful order event. Since #452 it also carries the drone's stock of the resource (`units`, the sum `holding()` reads) and `is_open` (sell: stock above the kept minimum; buy: stock below the wanted maximum), kept current whenever a holding or an order changes. The order books read only open orders, in price order, from `latest_orders_open` (resource, event_type, is_open, price); equal prices list the lowest drone ID first. The order-book queries (600, 601, 610, 611 and those built on them) read it, in place of `MAX(id) ... GROUP BY drone` over every order event for the resource.
* **Movable stock in the order books (#502).** Round 55 (#380) had 29,484 pulls refused with -5 ("the seller's order didn't allow selling that many"). The books were right as far as they went: a miner listed 1 to 3 metals, six buyers read the book within seconds of each other, and each pulled the same units. The first pull took them and the rest were refused when the events engine reached them, because a pull's events are inserted pending (outcome 0) when it is scheduled and settled later, and the books counted only settled stock. `latest_orders.units` also counted installed and locked units (a factory's inputs in a job), which a pull can't take: the pull's check reads the free stock (installed 0, locked 0). The books (`orders()` and `movable()` in `build_game_data.py`; the API's `/orders` through `ApiController::movable`) now list each order's movable stock: its free holding, less the drone's pending depletions for a sell order or plus its pending accumulations for a buy order (`events_inventory` index, outcome 0). `is_open` still narrows the books first; an order whose movable stock leaves nothing to trade isn't listed. On round 55's world a book took about the same time as before (about 1.1 ms against 1.0 ms warm).

Triggers on `events` keep both, after every insert, update and delete, whichever program wrote the event (the engines, the House market, refunds, wreck clean-up). They recompute the affected row by the same rule the queries used, the newest successful event of its kind by id (`refresh_holding` and `refresh_order`, each one or two steps down `events_inventory` or `events_orders`). So the tables can't drift from the history, whatever order events settle in. An update that doesn't touch what they record, most often setting an event's place, is skipped. Both tables cascade on `agents`, because deleting a drone deletes its events by cascade, which fires no trigger. As-of questions (query 615's hour-old board) still read `events`. Measured on a settled 1,000-ship world (56,000 events; the same saved state, 5 real minutes each):
* the order-book queries fell from 9.6 ms to 2.3 ms a call (101 s of MySQL time to 28 s);
* the engines settled 53% more events (51,629 against 33,636) and ended 11 s behind instead of 16 s.

The clock still fell to 427× in both runs: the game engine's query for due commands is now the top cost (#432). On round 41's aged 183-drone world #430 made no difference, since #427 had already made its order books cheap there; its top cost is the company's latest event (#435).

<a name="Load-Test"></a>**The load test (#429, part of #428).** `game-engine/tools/load-world.sh start <database> <dir>` builds a throwaway world from the checkout: a fresh database with the migrations and game data, the House stations, both engines at the live clock settings, and the House market runner. `php artisan forge:load-test N --starters=... --designs=chemist:12,...` releases starters and the example company designs. `forge:load-test --measure=SECONDS` samples the engines every `--every` seconds and reports:
* the median drone turns a minute, how far the engines fell behind, and how far the clock slowed;
* the game time that passed;
* the statements MySQL spent longest on (`performance_schema`).

It fails if a pass mark is missed (`--min-turns`, `--max-lag`, `--min-scale`) and appends a JSON line to `--history`. `.github/workflows/load.yml` runs it nightly, and by hand, on the round mix (60 starters and 45 company designs, with pass marks) and on 1,000 ships (recorded). It also runs briefly on pull requests that change the engines or the game-data queries; that run isn't a required check, because a slow runner can miss a mark.
* A drone's latest event before a game time, which the Event Engine asks for with every event, walked back through every drone's events by game time; index `events_drone_game_t` (drone, outcome, game_t). The latest sell or buy order of each drone (queries 600 to 612) took about 630 ms at day 90; index `events_orders` (event_type, outcome, resource, drone) brings it to about 4 ms.

**Pauses.** When it finds nothing to do, the Game Engine sleeps `DB_READ_TIME` and the Event Engine `ENGINE_CHECK_TIME`, both 1 second since #380 (5 before). Each action passes through both engines, so a drone could wait up to about 10 real seconds per action just on these pauses, with both engines nearly idle: at 600 game seconds a second that was over an hour and a half of game time. On a live server at 24, 10 seconds is 4 game minutes; 1 second costs a few cheap queries a second.

<a name="Settlement-in-the-game-engine"></a>**Settlement inside the game engine (#694 stage 4 step 2, `EVENTS_IN_ENGINE`).** Stage 4 merges the two engines, toward keeping the world in the engine's memory. Step 1 linked both into one program (the events engine's clashing names carry an `ee_` prefix). Step 2, behind `EVENTS_IN_ENGINE` (default 0: as before), lets the game engine settle. With it at 1:
* **Who settles.** Shard 0's game engine runs the events engine's pass (`ee_pass`: a batch of due events, then the stations, the share exchange, the processor tax and the archive when due) between turns (`settle.c`). `bin/events-engine` neither settles nor takes the lock: it logs once that the game engine settles and rests, since an engine that exits is restarted by Docker again and again.
* **Its own connection.** Settlement uses the events engine's own database connection, opened by the game engine beside the turns' one, and runs only after a turn has committed or rolled back and given up its event-order locks. So settlement and turns never share a transaction, and one thread never waits on its own row locks. The shared modules (`db_common.c`, `game_time.c`, `orbits.c`) ask `engine_get_db_connection()`, which returns the settlement connection while a pass runs (`engine_set_settlement_connection`) and the turns' otherwise; the events engine's own files already asked for theirs.
* **One settler.** The pass takes the events engine's named lock (`events-engine`, `db_claim_engine`) on its connection, without waiting, and tries again every 5 seconds until it has it: a `bin/events-engine` still stopping may hold it. Nothing settles meanwhile.
* **When.** About every 50 real milliseconds, at once after a batch came back full (more is due), but never sooner after a pass than that pass took while turns wait, so settlement takes at most about half the engine when both have work (`settle_clock.c`, tested by `test_settle_clock.c`). With no turns to run, the engine settles while it rests, back to back while batches come back full.
* **The log.** Settlement's lines, the events engine's and the shared modules' during a pass, go to the game engine's log starting `[events]`.

On its own the step gains nothing (the same work, in one process); later steps keep due events and drones' wakes in memory.

<a name="Due-events-from-memory"></a>**Due events from memory (#694 stage 4 step 3, #432, `EVENT_HEAP`).** Each pass used to ask MySQL for the first due, pending events (`SELECT * FROM events ... WHERE game_t <= now AND outcome = 0 AND action not aborted ORDER BY game_t, action, id LIMIT batch`): the costliest statement on the live database. With `EVENT_HEAP=1` (default 0: as before), which needs `EVENTS_IN_ENGINE=1` (otherwise the engine logs a NOTICE and the heap stays off; `bin/events-engine` never uses it), settlement keeps those events in memory instead:
* **The heap.** A min-heap (`event_heap.c`, tested by `test_event_heap.c`) of every pending event's id and key, ordered exactly as that query's `ORDER BY`: `game_t`, then `action` (NULL first, as MySQL sorts it), then `id`. An index by id keeps each event in once.
* **A pass** takes the due events (key `game_t` <= now) from the top, at most a batch, reads just their rows with `WHERE id IN (...)` under the query's other conditions and its order, and settles them exactly as before. An event whose row has gone, has been settled since (outcome != 0: the game engine aborted its action) or whose action is aborted is dropped, as the query would have skipped it. After the batch commits, any event still pending (a passing failure) goes back in, as the query would read it again; after a rollback, the whole batch goes back.
* **How it stays complete.** At start, one read of every pending event (the query without its due time and `LIMIT`). The events the program inserts itself, the game engine's turns (`protocol_flush_events` via `db_insert_event`) and settlement's follow-ups (refunds, fuel, station and wreck events via `db_insert_event_e`), are staged with their id (`mysql_insert_id`) and go into the heap when their connection commits, and are forgotten if it rolls back. Each pass first reads the events with ids past the highest it has seen (a short primary-key range) and adds the pending ones not in yet: other shards' turns, and anything another program inserts (the website inserts none pending today; House orders are inserted settled). So a pass sees what the batch query would have. An auto-increment id is taken before its transaction commits, so an event can commit after a higher id was seen: the ids that read skipped are looked up again each pass for 10 real seconds, and a sweep each real second reads again the ids of the last 10 to 20 real seconds. Every five real minutes the full read runs again as a check. Once a real minute the log says (NOTICE) how many are pending, taken and dropped, and how many each read found that this engine hadn't pushed.
* **What it costs.** Memory: about 16 bytes an event plus the index, a few MB for 100,000 pending. A stale entry is harmless (dropped when taken); a missing one would wait for the sweep or the five-minute check.

Settlement keeps its order: the same events, in the same order. On local load worlds (the CI round mix), a check build that ran the old query beside the heap on every pass found the two batches the same on all 3,456 passes with one game engine and all 4,151 with two (`ENGINE_SHARDS=2`); before the skipped ids were looked up each pass, two engines differed on 1 pass in 3,000, by one event that had committed late. An event whose transaction commits more than 10 real seconds after its id was skipped waits for the sweep (up to a second) or the five-minute check. At start the sweep also looks 4,096 ids below the top id it read, so an event whose transaction was still open while the start's read ran (another shard's turn, say) is found within a second, not at the five-minute check (#917: the first live restart with both heaps on found 114 such events at the five-minute check). The wake heap does the same for commands. The five-minute check itself raised a false alarm (#946: about 100 "not in the heap" each time on local load worlds set up as live, with step 6's flag on or off): it ran before the pass's reads, so it counted as missing the events inserted since the last pass, which the read past the highest id would have taken a moment later (on a 1,000-ship world, 133 events, all among the newest 170 ids), and it moved the highest id seen past ids it hadn't noted, so any of those not committed yet waited for the sweep. Now the check runs after the pass's reads and reads only ids up to the highest seen, and only the read past the highest id moves it. And a skip wider than 256 ids (a burst: a big release, a busy second), which used to go unnoted and wait for the sweep, is kept as a range and read again each pass for the same 10 seconds. The wake heap does both the same way.

<a name="Drone-wakes-from-memory"></a>**Drone wakes from memory (#694 stage 4 step 4, #432, `WAKE_HEAP`).** Each second (and whenever its batch ran out) a game engine asked MySQL for the next 64 commands to run: three queries over every due command of its shard (the House's station drones' first, then a loading drone's commands but its resumes, then the rest, each by id), each skipping a command held back (a resume whose drone's action still has events to settle; a new script or `<launched>` while the drone's running script has a resume queued). On the live server that was the "next command" statement: about 27 ms and 383 rows a call. With `WAKE_HEAP=1` (default 0: as before; it needs no other flag) each game engine keeps its own shard's commands in memory instead (`wake_heap.c`, `db_wake.c`):
* **Where a command waits.** Each command's id, drone and due time (`deliver_at`), in one of: *waiting*, a min-heap by the game time it may run from; *ready*, a min-heap keyed (tier, id) exactly as the queries order them (tier 0 for a station drone's command, 1 for a loading drone's (no `release_vm`) commands but its resumes, 2 for the rest); *untimed*, a player's command the light-speed stamping hasn't timed yet; *recheck*, held for another reason, looked at next batch as the query would. An index by id keeps each command in once: a new key replaces the old one.
* **A batch.** Commands now due move from waiting to ready. The ready ones are taken in key order and checked a chunk at a time by one query on their ids, with the batch queries' own conditions as a column (`db_runnable_where_at`), as of the same game time. A runnable one goes in the batch when it reaches the top; one not due yet (a delayed resume) goes back to wait for its time; one whose drone has changed tier (it launched) goes back with its real key; one held otherwise (a new script while a resume is queued) goes to recheck; one whose row has gone is dropped as stale. Keys are never later than the truth, only earlier, so this gives the queries' exact batch. Each command is still read by id just before it runs, as before.
* **How it stays complete.** At start, one read of the shard's commands. The engine's own writes are staged and applied when their transaction commits, forgotten if it rolls back: the resume a turn inserts, `<launched>` queued for a drone's arrival or a new drone (settlement's launch, on its own connection), the command run and deleted, resumes deleted (`<abort>`, a launch) or delayed (processor tax), and a drone's turn, which sends its held commands to recheck. Stamping (shard 0, outside a transaction) moves a command at once. Before each batch, the engine reads the commands with ids past the highest it has seen, every shard's, keeping its own (the website's releases, pushes and mail; other programs'), then looks up again the ids that read skipped (not committed yet), for 10 real seconds, and the untimed commands (another shard's stamping); once a real second it sweeps the last 10 to 20 real seconds of ids. The skipped ids are looked up every batch, as settlement's are each pass: a release's script and its `<launched>` commit together, but a turn's resume can take an id between them, so the read past the highest id finds `<launched>` at once while the script's ids were skipped. Looked up only once a real second, the script came up to a second later, `<launched>` ran first on a drone with no script loaded, and `launched()` never ran (the first CI run with `WAKE_HEAP=1`: the docker smoke test's pushed drone never ran its first code; `game-engine/tools/release-order-check.sh`, run by the load test, found 10 to 16 of 40 releases so). Every five real minutes the start's read runs again, with a WARNING if it finds any. Once a real minute a NOTICE gives the counts. A command deleted by the website (a new release, a wound-up company, a world reset) is dropped when it is next checked.
* **Held resumes.** A resume held for its drone's action waits until that action's next pending event is due, less a real second of game time: an action is aborted, or its events settle, only from then (game time moves a real second at a time, and another engine's clock may tick a moment sooner). Then, each batch, one grouped query on those actions' pending events (by `events.action`) asks whether each still has any; while it has, the resume waits for the next one, and once it hasn't (or the action is aborted), the resume is checked in full. The query's hold for a resume is exactly that, while the drone's `waiting_action` stays the same, and only the drone's own turn changes that: a turn sends its held commands back to be checked.
* **What it costs.** A few tens of bytes a command. The checks are primary-key lookups; a held resume costs one indexed lookup of its action a batch while its action's events are due and unsettled, as the query's hold clause did.

The batches keep their order. A check build that, on every batch, also ran the old queries just before and just after the heap and compared, found on local load worlds (the CI round mix) every batch the same as the queries' with one game engine (three runs of about 600 batches each). With two (`ENGINE_SHARDS=2`, each engine reading at `READ COMMITTED`) a batch can differ when another program commits between the statements being compared: in the round mix 2 of about 1,230 differed, by resumes whose action settlement had just finished; with 1,000 ships and settlement and due events in the game engine (as live), shard 0 matched on all of over 400 batches and shard 1 on 795 of 833, the other 38 differing only by commands shard 0's stamping had timed during the comparison, with the commands both had in the same order every time. Such a command is in the next batch.

On the local load worlds there was no gain to measure, and one setting may be worse. The old batch queries cost little there: about 3.7 ms a call and 2,400 rows (644 calls in a 10-minute 1,000-ship run, against 25 s of `COMMIT`s), not the live server's 27 ms. What replaces them cost about as much: in a 1,000-ship run the held resumes' action lookups took 2.3 s (1,188 calls of about 2 ms), the chunk checks 0.5 s and the reads of new and recent ids 0.7 s. Turns a minute moved more between runs of one setting than between settings (round mix, 6 minutes' warm-up, 3 measured; median turns a minute, most behind): one engine 1,941 and 1.9 s off, 1,782 and 2.0 s on; two engines 1,839 and 1.2 s, 1,815 and 2.1 s off, 1,865 and 2.0 s on. With 1,000 ships on two engines and settlement and due events in the game engine (as live), turns a minute were alike (4,600 and 4,648 off; 4,408 and 4,663 on) but the clock slowed further with it on (to 1,350x and 1,012x off; 759x and 570x on), perhaps because shard 0's lookups delay settlement between turns: worth watching on a live trial. An earlier build that checked held resumes in full each batch ran the round mix on two engines at about 1,500-1,540 turns a minute; asking only about their actions brought it back.

<a name="Holdings-in-memory"></a>**Holdings in memory (#694 stage 4 step 5, #431, `MEMORY_STATE`).** `units()` (query 212) is the drones' most frequent question: a sum over a drone's `holdings` rows for one resource, one statement a call. With `MEMORY_STATE=1` (default 0: as before; it needs no other flag) each game engine keeps a copy of the whole `holdings` table in memory (`holding_state.c`, `db_state.c`) and answers `units()` from it, with the column name MySQL gave the query's first answer. Only query 212 as the game data has it today is answered so; if its script changes, MySQL answers it again. The FORTH VM still asks MySQL.
* **The feed.** Holdings change whenever an event settles, and also when the website winds up a startup or resets the world, and in procedures (`refresh_holding`). Rather than find every writer, migration 0092 puts triggers on `holdings` itself: each insert, update that changes the units, and delete writes the row's key and its units after the change (NULL: deleted) to `holding_changes`, in the writer's own transaction. They write only while `engine_feeds` says so: an engine with the flag on turns it on at start, and shard 0 with the flag off turns it off and empties the table, so nothing grows while the flag is off. The flag is read `FOR SHARE`, so switching it waits for writers that read it before, and every later writer sees it. While it is on, shard 0 deletes changes over two real minutes old.
* **How the copy stays the table.** At start, between turns, one read of `holdings` in a consistent snapshot, then the feed again from 5,000 ids before the top that snapshot saw (a change it couldn't see yet comes in; the others replay to what it read). A read of the feed takes the ids past the highest seen, then the ids earlier reads skipped (not committed yet) for 60 real seconds, and applies them in id order. A change applies to its row only over an older one, so one read late can't undo a newer one; a deleted row is kept as absent with its id. Every five real minutes the table is read again in a snapshot with the feed read in it, and compared: a WARNING gives how many rows differed, and the copy takes the table's rows. An engine that hasn't read the feed for 90 real seconds reads the table again. A NOTICE once a real minute gives the counts.
* **When the feed is read.** A turn's own statements never change holdings: it inserts events pending, and settlement settles them on its own connection, between turns. So nothing is staged per connection; every change comes through the feed. A turn's transaction reads the feed at its first `units()`: under REPEATABLE READ (one engine) that is the snapshot every statement of the transaction reads, so the answer is the one the SQL would give. In one engine that settles events itself (`EVENTS_IN_ENGINE=1`, `ENGINE_SHARDS=1`), only its own settlement changes holdings often, and only between turns, so a turn reads the feed only after a settlement pass. Every engine reads it once a real second between turns, for the other writers: a wind-up's or a reset's deleted holdings are seen up to a second late, as if they had committed then.
* **Several engines.** Each engine keeps every drone's holdings (a drone's `units()` asks about itself, but the copy costs little) and reads the one feed, so it works with any `ENGINE_SHARDS`; with more than one, settlement is another engine's, so each turn that asks reads the feed. Under READ COMMITTED (several engines) a statement sees what committed while the turn ran; the copy, read at the transaction's first `units()`, gives the same answer for the rest of that transaction, as REPEATABLE READ does with one engine.
* **What it costs.** About 35 bytes a holding in memory: a few MB for 100,000. Per holdings change, one primary-key read of `engine_feeds` even while the flag is off, and one extra row written while it is on (in the same transaction).
* **Measured** (local, 3-minute runs after 6 minutes' warm-up). Check builds that also asked MySQL each `units()` compared about 680,000 answers in three set-ups (one engine with the events engine apart; two engines as live; one engine settling itself): none differed, and no five-minute check found a row out of step. The round mix with one engine: 1,888 turns a minute off, 2,115 on. Two engines with steps 2–4 and 1,000 ships, twice each: 5,158 and 5,222 off, 4,798 and 4,526 on; the clock's lowest pace 570× off and 1,350× on both times; at most 16 and 12 seconds behind off, 13 and 9.9 on.

Left for later steps: the order books (600, 610, 615), which also read pending events, aborted actions, tool locks, credits and drones' places; settlement's own inventory lookups (`db_e.c`), which read its own uncommitted writes in the middle of a batch; and `hold()` (210) and `cargo_mass()` (204).

<a name="Engine-event-ids"></a>**The engine's own event ids (#694 stage 4 step 6, `ENGINE_OWNS_EVENTS`).** Step 7 will write behind: a turn's events will live in memory for a second or two before one flush writes them, so the engine must know an event's id before its row exists. With `ENGINE_OWNS_EVENTS=1` (default 0: as before; it needs no other flag) the engines take their events' ids themselves (`event_ids.c`, `db_reserve_event_ids` in `db_common.c`).
* **Who writes `events`.** The game engines' turns (`protocol_flush_events`, `db_insert_event`); settlement's follow-ups, in whichever engine settles (`db_insert_event_e`: trades, refunds, fuel, station and wreck events; and the failed transfer's fuel refund); settlement's start-up freeing of stuck tools (one `INSERT ... SELECT`); the website (House orders in `HouseMarket::post`, starting cargo in `Agent::addCargo` for releases, the House's stations and new companies); and the one-off migration 0067. The website deletes pending events in a world reset; the archive procedure moves old rows to `events_archive`. With the flag on, the first two take their ids as below; the rest keep `AUTO_INCREMENT`.
* **What depends on the ids.** Settlement's order, `(game_t, action, id)`, in the SQL and the heap, where the id only orders one action's events at one game time. The current-state triggers (migration 0032), which keep a drone's newest event of each kind by id in `holdings` and `latest_orders`, and the archive, which keeps the latest of each kind by `(game_t, id)`: so, per drone, ids must follow the order the events were written in, which `protocol_flush_events` turns into game-time order by starting an action after the drones' latest events. The "previous event" lookups (`ORDER BY id DESC`), the archive's windows (ids follow game time closely), `observations.event` and the other references, and the website's `EventHistory`, which reads both tables by id.
* **One counter.** So the ids come from the table's own `AUTO_INCREMENT` counter, shared with every writer, not from blocks per engine. A block reserved ahead would put an engine's later events below ids that other writers (another shard's turn touching the same drone, settlement, the website) took in between, and the triggers would then keep the older event. The engine reserves exactly the ids it is about to use, where the insert took them: a turn after its order locks and its delay, settlement just before its insert. A reservation inserts one placeholder row an id (`game_t` -1, every other column NULL) and rolls them back: InnoDB never hands out an auto-increment value twice, the rows of one statement get consecutive values (`innodb_autoinc_lock_mode` 2, "simple inserts"), and every writer after that gets a higher id. The events are then inserted with those ids. Any writer keeps `AUTO_INCREMENT` and needs no change.
* **Its own connection.** The placeholders go in on a connection of their own, at READ COMMITTED, with a lock wait of a second at most. The first build reserved inside the caller's transaction: under REPEATABLE READ (settlement's connection, and one game engine's) a rolled-back insert leaves gap locks on the end of every index until that transaction ends, so settlement's batch held the end of `events` and the website's releases deadlocked with it. A reservation that fails falls back to `AUTO_INCREMENT`, as with the flag off.
* **The log.** A NOTICE at the first reservation, and once a real minute the counts: events inserted with reserved ids, reservations and ids, inserts by `AUTO_INCREMENT`, failed reservations and duplicate ids (a WARNING each; never expected). A check build (`make CHECK_EVENT_IDS=1`) also counts whether each reservation's ids were consecutive and whether, after each insert, the drone already had an event with a higher id.
* **What it costs.** Two statements per reservation (the insert and the rollback), one reservation a turn that performs and one per settlement follow-up, and the placeholders' writes to every index of `events` and their undo. On the 1,000-ship load world about 3,000 reservations a minute in each engine, at about 1.5 ms of MySQL time each (the rollback about 0.8).

**Measured** (local load worlds; 6 minutes' warm-up, 3 measured). Check builds compared the two ways of taking ids directly. With two engines as live (steps 2–5 on, 1,000 ships), 86,837 events took reserved ids: every one of 35,470 reservations got consecutive ids, and 2 events came in below an id their drone already had (a settlement event of the other engine that took its id between a turn's reservation and its insert); with the flag off the same check found 4 of 101,508 events so with `AUTO_INCREMENT`, the same race between a turn and settlement, which share no lock. One engine with the events engine apart: 45,380 events, all consecutive, none below. The release-order check passed with the flag on in every run, and no reservation failed. Throughput: the round mix stayed at 1,800× and at most 2.1 s behind both ways; its median turns a minute swings with a burst at the start of the window (8,764 and 10,945 off, 1,880 and 10,225 on). As live, twice each: 893 and 4,864 turns a minute off, 4,850 and 4,572 on; at most 60.9 and 12.0 s behind off, 14.5 and 10.3 on; the clock's lowest pace 570× and 1,350× off, 570× and 1,012× on. The first run off had a minute of slow commits (4.5 ms each against 0.4) that the others hadn't. No gain, as expected, and a cost to watch: the reservations' rollbacks alone took about 14 s of MySQL's time in 3 minutes.

Nothing changes with the flag off: the statements are the same text, and no line is logged. The website, procedures and migrations are unchanged. "One writer" in the plan's sense, the website's House orders and starting cargo going through the engine, is left for step 7 to decide: those rows commit in the website's own transactions (a release's cargo with its drone and commands, #916's lesson), and they already take their ids from the same counter in the order they are written.


**Memory (#380).** Valgrind found the engines leaking: about 7 MB a real minute (the Game Engine reached 1.9 GB in 3 hours) and about 450 MB a game day (the Event Engine). `embed_free` (`embed_util.c`) cleared the VM struct before freeing its core, so every VM leaked 64 KB; three lookups (`db_get_agent_engine_info` after a command, after a rename, and for the light-delay query tag) loaded a VM they never freed; and `event_update_new_cargo_and_quantity` never freed the resource list it fetched. `db_scape_string` also wrote a byte past its buffer for empty input. All four are fixed; under valgrind both engines now lose nothing but a few small blocks.

<a name="Local-Sightings"></a>**Local sightings.** A drone sees an observable event when it happened where the drone is now, since the drone arrived there. The Event Engine records where an observable event happened (`events.place`, the object its drone was at when it was processed), and each drone keeps when it reached where it is (`agents.arrived_t`, game time, set by the triggers `agents_arrived_insert` and `agents_arrived_update` whenever a drone is made or its `object_id` changes, whatever makes the change; migration 0016). Query 803 and the website's event list work the sightings out when asked: `e.place = drone.object_id AND e.game_t >= drone.arrived_t AND e.game_t <= game now`. So a drone forgets what it saw at a place once it leaves, as it only sees live orders where it is. The `observations` table keeps the rows that are one per event: the acting or affected drone's, and the Central Database's (drone 0), which wait for the light to reach Earth.

Also: `db_reconnect` checks the connection at most every 5 seconds instead of before every query; the engines' logs are quiet at `APP_TRACE_LEVEL -1` (only lines with ERROR, WARNING or NOTICE; Docker's default), since every step logged at level 0 is gigabytes a day at full load, and rotated logs are removed after 7 days; and the Docker database runs without a binary log and flushes its log once a second (`--disable-log-bin --innodb-flush-log-at-trx-commit=2`): a thousand drones saving their 64 KB VMs wrote gigabytes of binary log an hour.

**Instruction metering.** A slice is measured in FORTH instructions, not seconds: the VM counts every instruction it runs, and the slice ends when the count reaches `MAX_CYCLE_INSTRUCTIONS`. Each `query` costs a flat `QUERY_INSTRUCTIONS` (1,000 by default) and each `perform` a flat `ACTION_INSTRUCTIONS` (1,000), since their real cost is database work. So the same script always runs the same instructions per slice, however fast the server is. Each drone's running total is kept in `agents.instructions`. The engine's own set-up code (loading the callback words, checking for a word) isn't metered. A drone still loading its released script (no `release_vm` yet) gets bigger slices: what is left of `LOAD_INSTRUCTIONS` (game-engine.conf, 20,000,000) in all, in one slice, but never less than a normal slice (`vm_meter_slice_budget`, `db_get_agent_loading`). In round 33, in 100,000-instruction slices, a 100-line script (8 to 9 million instructions) took about 80 turns, 1.4 to 1.7 game days at 600 game seconds a second, with about 3,000 slices a minute shared by 120 drones; now a release runs `launched` about 4 real seconds after it is queued. A loading drone's script pieces also go to the front of the command queue (`db_get_next_command` orders them first; its resumes don't), so a release doesn't wait a turn per piece behind every running drone (#380 round 34: a 7 KB test in a test world of 70 drones took 10 to 20 real minutes; now seconds). The test world stops a run's script `test_run_minutes` (config/forge.php, 30) real minutes after it started, when the next test starts (`TestWorld::stopExpired`: its queued commands go, its results stay). The VM reads about 80 million instructions a real second, so the longest load holds the engine for a quarter of a second, once. A script that loops at its top level uses up the allowance once and then gets normal slices.

<a name="Doubles"></a>**Double cells (#324 step 9).** Cells are 16 bits, so money and volumes are doubles: two cells, the high cell on top.

* **Words.** Every drone's VM gets the standard word library (`vm_library.c`), the standard Forth double-cell words: `um* um+` (in the VM's system words, which players don't see, so the library makes them visible), `s>d dnegate d+ d- dabs d0= d0< d= d< du< 2swap 2over d> dmax dmin m* m+ ud. d.`. `vm_add_callbacks` loads it, unmetered, into a VM that doesn't have `d.` yet. A saved VM keeps it, so it is added once.
* **Query results** with `d_` columns, and **query inputs** through `[dvalue_N]` tags (see [Queries](#Queries)).
* **Protocol parameters.** `protocols.double_params` is a bit mask (bit 0 = parameter 1) of the parameters taken off the stack as doubles: high cell first, then low (`protocol_read_parameters`). Sell (17) and buy (18) "double price" are protocols 7 and 8 with the price, parameter 3, a double (mask 4), for prices past 65,535. Migration 0008 adds the column. The protocols loader names its columns now, rather than `SELECT *`.
* **Tests.** `test_double.c` checks the words past 32,767 and 65,535.

<a name="Game-Words"></a>**Game words and starter scripts (#324 step 15).** After the double-cell words, every new drone's VM gets the game words (`vm_library_game_source` in `vm_library.c`, loaded by `vm_add_callbacks` once, when the word `stdlib` is missing, after the `perform` and `query` callbacks they use). They are ordinary definitions, so the language doesn't change; they make scripts readable:

* **Constants** for places (`earth` 3, `moon` 10, `mars` 4) and resources (`fuel`, `magmite`, `kunspar`, `yalspar`, `vladnium`, `wolfite`, `metals-a1` to `metals-a3`, `inorganics-a1`, `inorganics-a2`, `organics-a1`, `organics-a2`, `mining-kit` 5901, `factory-kit` 5902).
* **Actions**, each leaving `perform`'s result: `unpack` (25902 when a Factory Kit is aboard, else 25901; #380), `travel ( place )`, `mine ( mineral )` (protocol mineral + 10000), `refine ( mineral )` (+ 20000), `make ( resource )` (+ 10000), `wait ( minutes )`, `sell-order ( price keep resource )` (7), `buy-order ( price want resource )` (8), `push ( n resource drone )` (10), `pull ( n resource drone )` (11), `launch ( drone kit )` (12), `plunder ( wreck )` (34), `jettison ( n resource )` (1: Dump resource, which takes a quantity since #380, n units for 1 fuel, like push and pull; it is not called `dump` because eForth's `dump` prints memory).
* **Orders it remembers (#380 round 31):** `sell-order` and `buy-order` note each order they post (`order-note`: 16 slots of 3 cells, `my-orders`; the resource is the key, negated for a buy), and `?sell-order` and `?buy-order` skip a post whose price and limit match the note (`order-same?`), leaving 0 without an action. A drone can't read its own orders back (queries 600 and 610 leave them out), so the notes are the only record. `orders-mine` keeps them honest: a launched drone starts with a copy of its parent's memory but none of its orders, so the notes carry the drone they belong to (`my-orders-of`) and are cleared when `me` differs. The table is cleared when the library loads, since allotted memory isn't zeroed. `test_double` checks that every library line fits the VM's 80-column input: a longer one is cut short without an error, which first lost `?buy-order`.
* **Queries**, through a 120-byte buffer `answer`: `ask ( [n] id -- rows )`, `me` (205), `location` (202), `units ( resource -- n )` (212), `company` (201), `credits ( -- d )` (101), `deposit ( mineral -- n )` (640: units left here, 0 if none), `today ( -- day )` (207: the game day now, #380), `trip ( place -- hours )` (704: game hours to fly there from here, rounded up, -1 if unknown; #380), and `sell-all ( resource -- )`, which offers all of it to the station here.
* **Prices (#380):** `best-offer ( resource -- price drone n )` (the first row of query 610 that isn't the asking drone's own order: the cheapest other drone's sell order here, and the units it will sell above its kept minimum; 0 0 0 if none; until round 26 it could return the drone's own order).
* **Market and state machines (#380):** `best-bid ( resource -- price drone n )` (the first row of query 600 that isn't the drone's own: the highest buy order here from a buyer who can pay), `sell-price` and `buy-price ( resource -- price )` (the best buy order and the cheapest other sell order here, from the order book only, the House's station drones' included; 0 if none; until stdlib 13 they also read the House's listed price, `house-price`, query 633, which is gone), and a state machine: the variable `mode`, `become ( mode -- )` (sets it and zeroes the round count), `in? ( mode -- f )`, `tick` and `ticks ( -- n )`. The players' manual's "Market-aware drones" and `examples/companies/adaptive.fth` use them. Query 681, the House's price board, is gone: what the station drones pay shows on the public board, 680, like anyone's trades.
* **Trading here (#380):** `sell-here ( n resource -- ior )` pushes into the best buy order here (`best-bid`), at most what it holds, then the next, until n are sold; `buy-here ( n resource -- ior )` pulls from the cheapest sell order here (`best-offer`), then the next, until it has n. They read the order book once (query 610 or 600; `sell-here` reads `units` first, as the book fills the `answer` buffer) and go down it, skipping the drone's own orders, and skip a seller or buyer that refuses for the next (#380 round 35: the same small miner lot was tried again and again, and factories stalled for days); at most 6 tries, each an action. Both leave 0 when anything moved, else -12 (the variables `trade-n`, `trade-r`, `trade-me`, `trade-ior`, `trade-tries` and `trade-have` hold their state). The Lua library's `buy_here` and `sell_here` do the same. They trade with the House's station drones exactly as with other drones. `sell-to-station` and `buy-from-station` are older names for them, and `sell-all ( resource )` is `sell-here` of all it holds.
* **Arithmetic eForth lacks (#380):** `2* ( n -- n*2 )`, `2/ ( n -- n/2 )` and `*/ ( n1 n2 n3 -- n1*n2/n3 )`, whose product is a double, so `30000 3 4 */` gives 22,500.
* **Sales on a day (#380):** `sold ( resource days-ago place -- units )`, from query 682: for a good and a game day D days ago (0 today so far), by place, the units the House stations bought (`station_batches`), the units sold between players (`trades`) and the average price, an hour old like the price boards. A seller checks how fast a product sells before making more.
* **Recipes and a fuel floor (#380 round 36, stdlib 18):** `recipe ( resource -- rows )` (query 695: the first protocol that makes the resource, a row a resource it changes: protocol, resource, units, made positive, used negative, its tool 0, minutes) and `recipe-row ( i -- addr )`; `fuel-floor ( n -- )` (the variable `floor-fuel`): at or below n fuel, `make` (but for fuel), `mine`, `refine` and `push` leave -30 without acting, so a deep build or a selling spree leaves fuel to buy fuel with (Sven 2, round 36, sold its last 3 fuel in a build's failure path and became a wreck with 5,517 credits). `units`, `ask` and `answer` now come before the action words, which read the tank. With no floor set (0) the words don't query anything. The Lua library has `recipe(resource)` (a table) and `fuel_floor(n)` with the same rules.
* `stdlib` leaves the library's version, 18 (17 before `recipe` and `fuel-floor`, 16 before `buy-here` and `sell-here` skipped a refusing trader, 15 before messages were read as events, with 803, 804 and 844, and `crowd`, 14 before `broadcast`, 13 before `sold` read query 682 without its House column, 12 before `house-price` was taken out, 11 before `sell-here` and `buy-here`, when the station words were protocols 31 and 32, 10 before `sell-all` sold to buy orders, 9 before `best-offer` and `best-bid` skipped the drone's own orders, 8 before `inorganics-a2` and `organics-a2`, 7 before `sold`, 6 before `*/`, 5 before the market words, 4 before the kits, 3 before `trip`, 2 before `today`; a drone keeps the library it was released with).

Four **starter scripts** in `examples/starters/` (miner, refiner, trader, maker; the Found a startup page, `/release`, offers them as cards, `Release::STARTER_CARDS`, that load their code into the editor, with the Miner picked when the page opens and `?starter=name` picking another (#719: it opened on a "Hello from orbit" script); the Trader isn't offered under the new rules; `Release::STARTERS`, and the kit each needs, `Release::STARTER_KITS`) use these words, each with comments that explain it:

* **Miner** (Mining Kit): flies to the Moon, mines kunspar or wolfite 20 units at a time, whichever has more left there (round 29: copies all mined kunspar while wolfite, which makes 6 units from 18 against kunspar's 4 from 20, sat untouched), refines it and sells the metals and wolfite's Inorganics A2 (copies spread out: a third at the Moon, the rest at Phobos and Deimos, selling at the Mars Exchange). Since #380 it keeps standing sell orders for its metals, set halfway between the best buy order and the cheapest other sell order there (about 22, 33 and 45 at the Moon since the House buys at three quarters of value; 25, 37 and 50 before), and sells into the best buy orders once 16 are aboard. Its kit can't brew fuel: below 40 it buys 30 wherever it is cheapest (`buy-cheapest`: a drone's sell order or the House), and at Phobos and Deimos, which have no station, it flies to the Mars Exchange when nobody sells. It waits while a deposit refills. After 12 dry tries in a row at the Moon (`dry-tries`, in the starter's Settings with `low-fuel` and `grow-at`, the 7,000 credits at which it launches a copy; since round 31 it never grows below `grow-floor`: 5,500, so it wouldn't buy a hull with nothing left for the kit, and since round 35 6,500, so 1,500 are left to work with (round 35: a newcomer's copy left its company 191 credits, under its cash floor, and both drones idled for 20 game days; the Maker and Refiner, FORTH and Lua, have the same floor)) it moves to Phobos, but only if fewer drones work there than at the Moon (`crowd`); round 29's six tries sent it on a 10-day trip early. The Refiner does the same. Both bid for fuel where nobody sells it (`ask-for-fuel`: a buy order at 7, which sellers see in the market; no broadcast, since the order is the request), wait two hours for a courier, then fetch it from the market and stop the bid: round 29's fuel couriers had nobody to serve.
* **Refiner** (Mining Kit; #380): the Miner's plan with yalspar and vladnium, selling Metals (A1) and (A2), Organics (A1) and Inorganics (A1) and (A2) the same way: the factories' fuel and polymer inputs. (Before #380 it brewed fuel and sold it at 4 a unit.)
* **Maker** (Factory Kit; #380): at the Moon, buys 20 Metals (A1) at a time wherever they are cheapest, makes alloys and rods, and sells the rods 4 an hour where they pay best: at the Moon when the best bid there is within `trip-margin` (20) of what rods fetched at Earth yesterday (`board-price`, query 680), else 12 at a time at Earth (round 34: Makers that all flew to Earth got 84 to 140 a rod while the Moon Depot paid 158 to 168). It stops forging at `keep-fuel` (25; it forged to 10, and one ended a round with 4 fuel and no seller near), buys its fuel the same way, and since round 34 grows like the Miner: with `grow-at` (7,000) credits it buys a hull and a Factory Kit at the exchange where it is and launches a copy.
* **Trader:** works the Moon and doesn't mine (#367). It buys metals from other drones' sell orders there (the Miner's) at up to 5 under the Mars Exchange's price (25, 40 and 55; 23, 38 and 53 before #380), resting 2 game hours when nobody sells. With 60 metals aboard, or 20 after 24 looks at the market (a trader that buys a little at a time still sets off), it hauls them to Mars and sells them to the Mars Exchange, at most 15 of each metal an hour for up to 6 hours (a fuller batch pays less; what is left goes on the next trip), then comes back. It buys its fuel (20 before each trip) from the House at the exchanges. (Before #367 it waited for 60 fuel, which the Mars-side copies never reached, and for 40 metals it mostly mined itself: in a load test no trader sold anything in 23 game days.)

Each one can earn alone; the makers buy what the miners and refiners sell, and the Chemist design sells fuel to the miners. Found while running them: Mine yalspar (11110) yielded kunspar (from `economy.xlsx`); `build_game_data.py` now makes every mining protocol yield its own mineral.

**Loading a script is free (#380)**, and since round 33 quick (see Instruction metering). The VM reads text slowly: each word it reads (a name, a number, a comment's `\`) costs about 20,000 instructions, most of them looking the name up in the dictionary, and a comment line about 30,000. Compiled code then runs fast. Until a released drone first runs `launched` (when `release_vm` is saved) it is only reading its program, so `db_take_power_due` marks those instructions paid instead of billing them; `agents.instructions` still counts them. Billed at `POWER_INSTRUCTIONS`, a starter script cost 13 to 25 fuel to load, but a 150-line script cost more than the 100 fuel a drone starts with: in round 21 four newcomers' drones died before their `launched` ran. A clone gets the compiled program and reads nothing.

<a name="Fuel"></a>**Fuel (#324 step 8).** Fuel (A1), resource 2061 (mass 1), is burned two ways:

* **By actions.** Every protocol's game data has a resource effect at time 0 that uses up fuel from the drone's hold. If the fuel isn't there, the action fails like any missing input (`perform` leaves -2). The cost is per action, whatever the multiplier: pushing 50 units burns as much as pushing 1 (`protocol_process_resource_effect` doesn't multiply fuel effects). Each protocol's description ends "Burns N fuel."

  | Actions | Fuel |
  |:--|--:|
  | Unpack a kit (25901, 25902) | 0 |
  | Dump, sell and buy orders, push, pull, import, export, plunder, station offers and orders | 1 |
  | Mining (11xxx) (2 until #324 step 15) | 1 |
  | Refining, alloys, organics, polymers, fuel (21xxx, 12xxx) | 2 |
  | Parts (13xxx) | 3 |
  | Systems (14xxx) | 4 |
  | Travel (5001) | 5 |
  | Modules (15xxx) | 6 |
  | Ship kits (16xxx) | 8 |
  | Launch (12): 10 burned and 20 for the clone ([Cloning](#Cloning)) | 30 |
  | Wait (3) | 0 |

* **A failed push or pull costs nothing (#380 round 38).** When a transfer fails (the seller's order no longer has the units, the buyer's order filled first, a full hold...), `event_process_transfer` undoes both halves and `db_refund_fuel` gives back the fuel the action burned, as a fuel increment event (action 0). In round 38 about 95 drones at the Moon lost a fuel for every pull race they lost. A transfer's two halves must also be two drones' (`event_get_transfer_partner`): in a pull of fuel the buyer's own fuel cost is a fuel decrement too, and it was paired with the buyer's increment, so an over-pull of fuel delivered the fuel unpaid while the seller's half failed on its own (found by the round 38 smoke test).
* **By computing (power), until #544.** Since #544 `POWER_INSTRUCTIONS` is 0 and thinking burns no fuel: the company pays processor tax in credits instead ([Processor tax](#Processor-tax)). Before that, after each slice the Game Engine worked out the whole units of fuel the drone owed: (`agents.instructions` − `agents.power_paid`) ÷ `POWER_INSTRUCTIONS` (game-engine.conf, 200,000 since #380 round 37, 100,000 before; 0 makes computing free; `vm_meter_fuel_due`). It marks them paid and burns them with protocol 2, "Burn power" (`[N 2 perform]`, one effect using up N fuel). The engine plans that action itself (`protocol_burn_power`), and the script doesn't wait for it. The rest of the instructions carry over to the next bill. If the burn fails for lack of fuel (outcome -2), the events engine makes the drone a wreck (`db_wreck_drone`: `agents.wrecked_at`, `wreck_cause` = `fuel`), and a wreck runs no more commands. A burn that fails for any other reason only leaves that bill unpaid. Taking something out of the hold never fails the cargo-space check (`event_no_cargo_condition`), so a drone over its capacity can still burn fuel.

Fuel is made from Organics and Inorganics with protocol 12061, "Make Fuel (A1)": 1 Organics (A1) and 2 Inorganics (A1) make 30 Fuel (A1) with a Chemical Reactor in 2 game hours. Refining 20 magmite gives exactly those inputs. A new drone (registration, release) starts with `forge.starting_fuel` (100) in its hold, next to its packed kit (40 of hull 1's 1,000 cargo; 500 until migration 0029). It is loose in the hold, because a released script needs power while it loads, before the kit can be unpacked. A launched drone's hold starts empty: its owner must push it fuel before its computing bill comes due.

<a name="Wrecks"></a>**Wrecks and plunder (#324 step 12).** A drone becomes a wreck for good when it crashes too often in a row, or its company is eliminated for not paying its tax (and, until #544, when it couldn't pay its thinking's power bill in fuel; since then a drone that runs dry is only stranded: its actions fail with -2 until fuel reaches it) (`agents.wrecked_at`, `wreck_cause`). A wreck refuses commands and does nothing more, but everything aboard it stays there: its hold and its installed and locked parts. Another player's drone at the same place can plunder it once:

* **Protocol 34, "Plunder wreck":** `W 34 perform`, where W is the wreck's drone ID. It burns 1 fuel and takes 1 game hour; its resource effect is a plunder event (type 11) that carries W in `new_quantity`.
* **The events engine** (`db_plunder`, `db_wreck.c`) checks the event when its time comes: W must be a wreck, at the plunderer's `object_id`, not plundered yet (else outcome -15, `OUTCOME_NO_WRECK`). Whose wreck it is doesn't matter (#367 step 1; own-player wrecks were refused with -13 before). It then marks the wreck plundered (`plundered_at`, `plundered_by`), so a wreck is plundered once even if two drones try. The plunderer's share arrives by haul actions of its own, after the plunder event, so a script that counts its hold straight after `plunder` returns may see none yet (the smoke test waits for it).
* **Everything leaves the wreck:** one action of protocol 35, "Plunder haul" (which nobody performs), with a decrement event for each resource in the hold and among the installed and locked parts (the latest totals, as `sqInventory` reads them).
* **The plunderer gets its share** (`house_rules.plunder_share`, 50%) of each resource's total, rounded down; the odd part-unit comes too with the matching chance, fixed for each wreck and resource (`wreck_share`, `wreck.c`), so a single part comes away whole from about half of the wrecks. Each resource arrives as its own protocol 35 action with an increment event, which the events engine checks like any other: what doesn't fit in the hold fails with -3 and is lost. What the plunderer doesn't get is destroyed, so wrecks are a sink.
